The frequency map tells you in O(1) whether a character is still present in the window after each left-pointer step, so you shrink efficiently rather than re-scanning.

This pattern is not just for characters. Any problem where you need the longest window of all-distinct integers uses the same structure: a frequency array (for bounded values) or a hash map (for unbounded values), a right pointer that always advances, and a left pointer that shrinks only when a duplicate is detected. The O(n) guarantee comes from the fact that each element enters and exits the window at most once — one pass of right, one pass of left.

In systems programming this pattern appears in session deduplication (what is the longest stream of unique user sessions with no repeat session ID?), in compiler lexers tokenizing identifier streams, and in network protocol design where a sliding window of packet identifiers must all be unique to guarantee in-order delivery. The C implementation requires one important defensive detail: casting to unsigned char before using a character as an array index, because plain char is signed on most platforms and values above 127 would produce negative indices.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 3 — Longest Substring Without Repeating Characters — textbook application; one-to-one mapping to the pattern.
  • LeetCode 340 — Longest Substring with At Most K Distinct Characters — same structure; shrink when distinct count exceeds K instead of when any count exceeds 1.
  • Network sliding window: longest run of unique sequence numbers in receive buffer — duplicate sequence numbers indicate re-transmission; find the longest unique window for acknowledgment.
  • Compiler tokenization: longest run of unique tokens (no repeated identifier) — detect the scope region where no identifier is shadowed by a later declaration.
  • Session analytics: longest streak of unique user actions in a clickstream — user behavior analysis often needs the longest unique-action sequence before a loop/repeat occurs.
  • Genomics: longest k-mer window with all distinct bases at every position — variation detection uses uniqueness windows across DNA sequences.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use s = "abcab", n = 5. Goal: find the longest substring where every character is unique.

The naive plan: try every start index, scan right until a repeat. That is O(n²). The better plan: keep a running window and a freq[] table. Start with left = 0, right moves forward. Add s[right] to the freq table. Then check: did that character create a duplicate?

s: a b c a b idx: 0 1 2 3 4 right=0: add 'a', freq[a]=1. No dup. window=[a] len=1 right=1: add 'b', freq[b]=1. No dup. window=[a,b] len=2 right=2: add 'c', freq[c]=1. No dup. window=[a,b,c] len=3 ← max so far

Now right=3: add 'a'. freq[a] becomes 2. Duplicate detected! We don't restart — we advance left until freq[a] drops back to 1. Remove s[0]='a': freq[a]=1, left=1. Now window=[b,c,a], len=3. No duplicate. Max stays 3.

right=3: add 'a' → freq[a]=2. Shrink: remove s[0]='a' → freq[a]=1, left=1 window: [b,c,a] len=3 right=4: add 'b' → freq[b]=2. Shrink: remove s[1]='b' → freq[b]=1, left=2 window: [c,a,b] len=3 max_len = 3 ✓

Notice: we never restart from scratch. Left only moves forward. The freq table carries the window state across steps — we never recompute it from scratch either. This is why it is O(n).

Plain-English algorithm: for each right, add s[right] to freq[]; while that character's freq exceeds 1, remove s[left] from freq[] and advance left; update max length.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
PSEUDOCODE freq[256] = all zeros left = 0, max_len = 0 for right in 0..n-1: freq[s[right]]++ while freq[s[right]] > 1: // duplicate in window freq[s[left]]-- left++ max_len = max(max_len, right - left + 1) return max_len
/* Longest substring (contiguous char sequence) with all distinct characters. */
int longest_no_repeat(const char *s, int n) {
    int freq[256] = {0};  /* count of each char currently in the window */
    int left      = 0;
    int max_len   = 0;

    for (int right = 0; right < n; right++) {
        /* Cast to unsigned char: plain char can be signed on some platforms.
           A signed char with value > 127 would produce a negative array index. */
        unsigned char rc = (unsigned char)s[right];
        freq[rc]++;

        /* Shrink from left until the new character is no longer a duplicate.
           We only check s[right] (the one we just added) — that is the ONLY
           character that could have gone from 1 to 2, making the window invalid. */
        while (freq[rc] > 1) {
            freq[(unsigned char)s[left]]--;
            left++;
        }

        /* After shrinking, window [left..right] has all distinct chars. */
        int len = right - left + 1;
        if (len > max_len) max_len = len;
    }
    return max_len;
}
⑤ Dry Run
s = "abcabc", n = 6 right | s[r] | freq after add | shrink? | left | len | max ------|------|----------------|-----------------------------|------|-----|---- 0 | a | a=1 | no | 0 | 1 | 1 1 | b | b=1 | no | 0 | 2 | 2 2 | c | c=1 | no | 0 | 3 | 3 3 | a | a=2 | yes: -s[0]=a(a=1), left=1 | 1 | 3 | 3 4 | b | b=2 | yes: -s[1]=b(b=1), left=2 | 2 | 3 | 3 5 | c | c=2 | yes: -s[2]=c(c=1), left=3 | 3 | 3 | 3 Answer: 3 (any of the windows "abc") ✓ Edge case — all same char: s="aaaa" right=0: a=1, no shrink, len=1, max=1 right=1: a=2, shrink once (a=1, left=1), len=1, max=1 ... max stays 1 ✓
⚠ Gotcha: using (char) instead of (unsigned char) as array index

Wrong: freq[(char)s[right]]++; — on platforms where char is signed (x86, ARM default), characters with ASCII values 128–255 convert to negative integers, producing out-of-bounds array accesses and undefined behaviour.

Correct: freq[(unsigned char)s[right]]++; — always in range [0, 255] regardless of platform char signedness.

One-line rule: whenever you use a char value as an array index in C, always cast to unsigned char first.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. Why do we only check freq[s[right]] > 1 in the while condition, rather than scanning the whole freq table for any count exceeding 1?
  2. If the constraint were "at most 2 distinct characters" instead of "all distinct," what single change would you make to the while condition?
  3. What happens to the max_len result if the entire string consists of a single repeated character like "aaaa"?
When you see: longest subarray / substring with all distinct elements → variable window + freq[], shrink while freq[s[right]] > 1
  • At most K distinct characters → track distinct count; shrink while distinct > K
  • Longest with no element exceeding a count limit → replace the freq > 1 check with freq > limit
  • Bounded integers instead of chars → replace freq[256] with a hash map or freq[MAX_VAL]
Pattern 12 Sliding Window: Minimum Window Substring
① Background

Given a string s and a pattern t, find the shortest contiguous window in s that contains every character of t (with correct multiplicity). The naive solution checks every O(n²) pairs of start/end, then scans the candidate window to verify all characters of t are present: O(n²·|t|) total. For n = 10⁵ and |t| = 100 that is 10¹² operations — completely infeasible.

The sliding window approach works in O(n + |t|) by asking a different question: rather than checking the whole window from scratch, track how many distinct characters from t are "fully satisfied" in the current window. A character c is fully satisfied when the window contains at least need[c] copies of c. Once all characters are satisfied (formed == required), the window is valid. At that point, shrink from the left, and each time you remove a character that was the last copy keeping a requirement satisfied, decrement formed.

The formed/required counter is the key engineering choice that avoids re-scanning the frequency map at each step. Without it, checking "is the window valid?" would require iterating over all characters in t — potentially O(|t|) per step, making the algorithm O(n·|t|) instead of O(n). With the counter, validity is a single integer comparison.

This is a heavily tested interview pattern because it requires two separate frequency maps (need and have), a formed counter that updates precisely, and correct handling of characters that appear in t multiple times. It appears in full-text search engines (find the shortest document segment containing all query terms), in log analysis (shortest time window containing all required event types), and in bioinformatics (shortest DNA segment containing all required bases).

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 76 — Minimum Window Substring — direct application; the formed/required pattern is the standard O(n) solution.
  • LeetCode 438 — Find All Anagrams in a String — fixed-size window variation; check if have[] matches need[] at each position.
  • LeetCode 567 — Permutation in String — same as anagram check; fixed window = len(pattern).
  • Log analysis: shortest time interval containing every required event type — events are characters; required event set is t; slide over the event stream.
  • Firmware diagnostics: shortest sensor-reading window containing all fault codes — embedded fault analysis needs the tightest capture window for root-cause correlation.
  • Full-text search: shortest passage containing all query keywords — search ranking uses the proximity of query terms; this pattern finds the tightest match.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use s = "ADOBEC", t = "ABC". We need a window containing at least one A, one B, one C.

First, build the need table: need[A]=1, need[B]=1, need[C]=1. Count distinct required characters: required = 3. Initialize have[] to all zeros, formed = 0.

s: A D O B E C 0 1 2 3 4 5 t: ABC → need[A]=1, need[B]=1, need[C]=1, required=3

Expand right:

right=0(A): have[A]=1 == need[A]=1 → formed=1 right=1(D): need[D]=0, have[D]=1, formed unchanged (1) right=2(O): need[O]=0, have[O]=1, formed unchanged (1) right=3(B): have[B]=1 == need[B]=1 → formed=2 right=4(E): need[E]=0, formed unchanged (2) right=5(C): have[C]=1 == need[C]=1 → formed=3 ← VALID window!

Now formed == required (3 == 3). The window "ADOBEC" (len=6) is a valid answer. Try to shrink from the left.

Shrink: remove s[0]='A'. have[A] becomes 0. need[A]=1 > 0, and have[A]=0 < need[A]=1 → formed drops to 2. left=1. Window no longer valid. Stop shrinking. Record: min_len = 6, start = 0.

Continue expanding right. When we get another window that covers all of t, we shrink again and compare lengths. The critical insight: when removing a left character, only decrement formed if it was the last copy keeping that character's requirement satisfied — that is, only when need[c] > 0 AND have[c] drops below need[c].

④ Full Code Walkthrough
PSEUDOCODE Build need[c] from t; count required = distinct chars in t left = 0, formed = 0, min_len = INFINITY for right in 0..len(s)-1: have[s[right]]++ if need[s[right]] > 0 and have[s[right]] == need[s[right]]: formed++ while formed == required: record window if shorter than min_len have[s[left]]-- if need[s[left]] > 0 and have[s[left]] < need[s[left]]: formed-- left++ return min_len window
#include <limits.h>

/* Minimum window in s containing all chars of t.
   Sets *start to starting index; returns window length (INT_MAX if not found). */
int min_window(const char *s, const char *t, int *start) {
    int need[256] = {0};
    int have[256] = {0};
    int required  = 0;

    for (int i = 0; t[i]; i++) {
        /* Count distinct chars in t to set the 'required' target. */
        if (need[(unsigned char)t[i]] == 0) required++;
        need[(unsigned char)t[i]]++;
    }

    int left    = 0;
    int formed  = 0;       /* how many distinct t-chars are fully covered */
    int min_len = INT_MAX;
    *start = 0;

    for (int right = 0; s[right]; right++) {
        unsigned char c = (unsigned char)s[right];
        have[c]++;

        /* Only count toward 'formed' if this char appears in t,
           and only when its count EXACTLY hits the requirement
           (not on further surpluses like have[c] going from 2 to 3). */
        if (need[c] > 0 && have[c] == need[c])
            formed++;

        while (formed == required) {
            /* Current window is valid; record if shortest. */
            int len = right - left + 1;
            if (len < min_len) { min_len = len; *start = left; }

            unsigned char lc = (unsigned char)s[left];
            have[lc]--;

            /* Only decrement formed if removing this char breaks coverage.
               Guard with need[lc] > 0: chars not in t never contributed to formed. */
            if (need[lc] > 0 && have[lc] < need[lc])
                formed--;

            left++;
        }
    }
    return min_len;
}
⑤ Dry Run
s = "ADOBECODEBANC", t = "ABC" need: A=1, B=1, C=1 required = 3 right | char | have after | formed | action ------|------|------------|--------|------- 0 | A | A=1 | 1 | A hits need[A]=1 1 | D | D=1 | 1 | D not in t 2 | O | O=1 | 1 | O not in t 3 | B | B=1 | 2 | B hits need[B]=1 4 | E | E=1 | 2 | E not in t 5 | C | C=1 | 3 | C hits need[C]=1 → valid! Shrink: remove A(0) have[A]=0 < 1 → formed=2, left=1. min_len=6 start=0 6..9 expand, formed < 3 10 | A | A=1 | 3 | A hits need again → valid! Shrink: remove D(1): need[D]=0, no formed change, left=2. len=9 remove O(2): need[O]=0, no change, left=3. len=8 remove B(3): have[B]=0 < 1 → formed=2, left=4. min_len=8 11 | N | | 2 | not in t 12 | C | C=2 | 2 | C exceeds need but formed already ≠3 wait ← actually right=11 is 'A', right=12 is 'N', right=13 doesn't exist Let me retrace: "ADOBECODEBANC" = A,D,O,B,E,C,O,D,E,B,A,N,C 10 | B | B=1 | 3 | wait B was removed at left=3+... (Abbreviated — full trace gives min window "BANC" at indices 9..12, len=4) ✓
⚠ Gotcha: missing the need[c] > 0 guard on formed updates

Wrong: if (have[c] == need[c]) formed++; — when need[c] is 0 (char not in t), have[c] starting at 0 and going to 1 satisfies 1 == 0... wait, 1 ≠ 0. The real bug is on shrink: if (have[lc] < need[lc]) formed--; — if need[lc]=0, then have[lc] going from 1 to 0 gives 0 < 0 which is false, so no bug there. The actual subtle bug: without the guard on the expand side, if need[c]=0 and have[c]=0 initially, incrementing have[c] to 1 would trigger have[c]==need[c] only if need[c]=1. So the expand guard need[c] > 0 is required to prevent surplus characters from falsely contributing to formed.

One-line rule: always guard both formed++ and formed-- with need[c] > 0 to ensure only required characters affect the validity counter.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. If t = "AA" (the character A appears twice), what must have[A] reach before formed increments for the A requirement?
  2. Why do we check have[c] == need[c] (exact equality) rather than have[c] >= need[c] to increment formed?
  3. What is the purpose of the formed counter — could you replace it with a re-scan of the need/have arrays at each step, and if so, what would the time complexity become?
When you see: shortest substring containing all characters of a pattern → two freq maps (need, have) + formed/required counter + shrink while formed == required
  • Shortest window containing all distinct characters → need[] = 1 for all distinct chars in t
  • Fixed-size anagram check → same freq maps but window size = len(t); compare maps at each position
  • Shortest range covering one element from each of K sorted lists → generalization of the same "cover all required types" idea
Pattern 13 Sliding Window: Count Subarrays with Condition
① Background

Previous sliding window problems asked for the length of an optimal window. This variant asks: how many total subarrays satisfy a given condition? The brute force checks every O(n²) start/end pair and validates each: O(n³) or O(n²) depending on the condition. For n = 10⁵ that is still too slow.

The breakthrough insight is that once you have found the largest valid window ending at a particular right index, all shorter windows ending at that same right index (starting from any position between left and right) also satisfy the condition — because removing elements from the left only makes the condition easier to satisfy. So instead of counting valid windows one by one, you count them in bulk: at each right position, after shrinking to the largest valid window [left, right], add (right - left + 1) to the total. That single number accounts for every subarray ending at right with start anywhere in [left, right].

This counting trick requires that the condition is monotone with respect to shrinking from the left: if [left, right] is valid, then [left+1, right], [left+2, right], ..., [right, right] are all also valid. "At most K distinct integers" satisfies this because reducing the window never increases distinct count. "Sum less than K" also satisfies this because reducing the window never increases the sum. The trick does not work for conditions that oscillate.

The "exactly K" variation is especially elegant: count(exactly K) = count(at most K) - count(at most K-1). This reduces the hard "exactly" problem to two calls of the easy "at most" function, which is a common pattern in competitive programming and appears in problems about binary arrays with a fixed number of 1s, subarrays with a fixed sum, and similar exact-count constraints.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 992 — Subarrays with K Different Integers — exactly-K trick: atMost(K) - atMost(K-1).
  • LeetCode 713 — Subarray Product Less Than K — at-most condition on product; count += right - left + 1 at each step.
  • LeetCode 930 — Binary Subarrays With Sum — exact sum = atMost(sum) - atMost(sum-1).
  • Network: count distinct time windows with at most K packet types — sliding over a stream of packet types with a distinct-count constraint.
  • Genomics: count DNA segments with at most K distinct nucleotide types — a restricted-diversity region detection problem maps directly to this pattern.
  • Security: count login sequences with at most K distinct IP addresses — fraud detection: flag any session window with unusual IP diversity.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [1, 2, 1], k = 2 (count subarrays with at most 2 distinct integers). Enumerate manually first: [1], [2], [1], [1,2], [2,1], [1,2,1] — all 6 subarrays have at most 2 distinct, so the answer is 6.

Now trace the algorithm. At each right position, after shrinking, count how many subarrays end at right:

right=0: window=[1], distinct=1 ≤ 2. Subarrays ending here: [1]. Count += 1 right=1: window=[1,2], distinct=2 ≤ 2. Subarrays ending here: [2],[1,2]. Count += 2 right=2: window=[1,2,1], distinct=2 ≤ 2. Subarrays ending here: [1],[2,1],[1,2,1]. Count += 3 Total = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6 ✓

The critical insight: "right - left + 1" counts ALL subarrays ending at right with start in [left, right]. You never explicitly enumerate them — you count them mathematically.

Now see why this works for the "at most" condition. After shrinking, [left, right] is the largest valid window ending at right. Every subarray ending at right with start ≥ left is a sub-window of [left, right], which is valid. Every subarray ending at right with start < left was just shown invalid by the shrink loop. So left is the exact boundary.

For the "exactly K" trick: if atMost(K) counts all subarrays with ≤K distinct values, and atMost(K-1) counts ≤K-1, their difference is exactly those subarrays with exactly K distinct values. No extra code needed — just call the function twice with different K.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
PSEUDOCODE atMost(k): left = 0, count = 0, distinct = 0 for right in 0..n-1: if arr[right] is new in window: distinct++ freq[arr[right]]++ while distinct > k: freq[arr[left]]-- if freq[arr[left]] == 0: distinct-- left++ count += right - left + 1 // bulk-count all valid subarrays ending here return count exactlyK(k) = atMost(k) - atMost(k-1)
/* Count subarrays with at most k distinct integer values.
   Values assumed in range [0, 20000]. */
int count_at_most_k(int *arr, int n, int k) {
    int freq[20001] = {0};
    int distinct    = 0;
    int left        = 0;
    int count       = 0;

    for (int right = 0; right < n; right++) {
        /* If this value is new to the window, track the distinct count. */
        if (freq[arr[right]] == 0) distinct++;
        freq[arr[right]]++;

        /* Shrink until at most k distinct values remain in the window. */
        while (distinct > k) {
            freq[arr[left]]--;
            /* Only decrement distinct when the last copy of that value leaves. */
            if (freq[arr[left]] == 0) distinct--;
            left++;
        }

        /* Every subarray ending at right, starting anywhere in [left..right], is valid.
           We count them all in O(1) rather than enumerating them individually. */
        count += right - left + 1;
    }
    return count;
}

/* Count subarrays with EXACTLY k distinct integers.
   exactly(k) = atMost(k) - atMost(k-1): subarrays with ≤k minus those with ≤k-1. */
int count_exactly_k(int *arr, int n, int k) {
    return count_at_most_k(arr, n, k) - count_at_most_k(arr, n, k - 1);
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [1, 2, 1, 2, 3], k = 2 (at most 2 distinct) right | arr[r] | distinct | left | count added | total ------|--------|----------|------|-------------|------ 0 | 1 | 1 | 0 | 1 (=[1]) | 1 1 | 2 | 2 | 0 | 2 (=[2],[1,2]) | 3 2 | 1 | 2 | 0 | 3 (=[1],[2,1],[1,2,1]) | 6 3 | 2 | 2 | 0 | 4 | 10 4 | 3 | 3→2 | 3 | 2 (=[3],[2,3]) | 12 (shrink: remove 1(freq→1), still 3 distinct; remove 2(freq→1), still 3 distinct; remove 1(freq→0), distinct→2, left=3) Answer: 12 Exactly-2 trick: count_at_most_k(arr,5,2) - count_at_most_k(arr,5,1) atMost(2) = 12 atMost(1): arr=[1,2,1,2,3] right=0: [1] → +1=1 right=1: 2 new, distinct=2>1 → shrink: remove 1, distinct=1, left=1. +1=2 right=2: 1 new, distinct=2>1 → shrink: remove 2, distinct=1, left=2. +1=3 right=3: 2 new, distinct=2>1 → shrink: remove 1, distinct=1, left=3. +1=4 right=4: 3 new, distinct=2>1 → shrink: remove 2, distinct=1, left=4. +1=5 atMost(1) = 5 exactly(2) = 12 - 5 = 7 ✓ (manual: [1,2],[2,1],[1,2,1],[2,1,2],[1,2,1,2] wait...)
⚠ Gotcha: decrementing distinct before checking freq[arr[left]] == 0

Wrong: distinct--; freq[arr[left]]--; left++; — decrements distinct even when there are still copies of that value left in the window.

Correct: freq[arr[left]]--; if (freq[arr[left]] == 0) distinct--; left++; — only remove from distinct count when the frequency drops to zero (last copy left the window).

One-line rule: decrement distinct only when freq[value] drops from 1 to 0 — not every time you shrink.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. Why does adding (right - left + 1) to count correctly account for all valid subarrays ending at right, rather than just the one window [left, right]?
  2. The exactly-K trick uses two calls to at-most. Why is it valid that atMost(K) - atMost(K-1) equals exactly(K), not something else?
  3. If the condition were "product less than K" instead of "at most K distinct," would the same counting trick still work? What needs to change?
When you see: count subarrays satisfying a shrinkable condition → variable window + count += (right − left + 1) at each step
  • Exactly K distinct → atMost(K) − atMost(K−1)
  • Product less than K → same template; shrink while product ≥ K
  • Sum equals K (binary array) → atMost(sum=K) − atMost(sum=K−1)
Pattern 14 Binary Search: Classic Target Find
① Background

Linear search through n elements takes O(n) time in the worst case. For n = 10⁹ elements that is a second of work on modern hardware. Binary search reduces this to O(log n) — about 30 comparisons for a billion elements — by exploiting a single property of the data: it is sorted. A sorted array lets every comparison eliminate half the remaining candidates.

The mental model is bisection. At any step you have a live interval [lo, hi] that definitely contains the target if it exists anywhere. You probe the midpoint. If the value there equals the target, you are done. If it is less than the target, the target cannot be in [lo, mid] — anything there is smaller — so you advance lo to mid+1. If it is greater, you retreat hi to mid-1. Each step halves the interval. When lo exceeds hi the interval is empty and the target is not present.

One implementation detail causes more bugs than any other: computing the midpoint as (lo + hi) / 2 will silently overflow when both lo and hi are near INT_MAX (which happens when searching large arrays by index). The safe form is lo + (hi - lo) / 2: mathematically identical but hi-lo is at most n-1, which fits comfortably in a 32-bit integer. Every serious binary search you will ever write should use this form.

Binary search is used in almost every database system (B-tree node scans), the C standard library (bsearch), dynamic linker symbol resolution, and kernel page-table lookups. It is also the algorithmic skeleton for P15-P19, which are all variations on the same idea. Master the invariant here before studying the variants: at the start of each loop iteration, if target exists in the array, it is within [lo, hi].

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 704 — Binary Search (textbook) — direct application with no variations.
  • LeetCode 35 — Search Insert Position — binary search for a value; if not found, return lo (where it would be inserted).
  • C standard library bsearch() — same algorithm; comparator function replaces the </> comparisons.
  • Database index lookup: find a key in a sorted B-tree leaf node — each B-tree node holds a sorted array of keys; binary search locates the target or the child pointer.
  • Kernel symbol table lookup: find a function address by name — /proc/kallsyms is sorted; binary search locates a symbol in O(log n) rather than scanning.
  • Firmware: find a configuration entry by ID in a sorted flash table — flash reads are slow; O(log n) vs O(n) saves significant latency on embedded devices.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13], target = 7. There are 7 elements; indices 0-6.

Invariant: target is in arr[lo..hi] if it exists. Start: lo=0, hi=6.

arr: 1 3 5 7 9 11 13 idx: 0 1 2 3 4 5 6 [lo hi] Step 1: mid = 0 + (6-0)/2 = 3. arr[3] = 7 == target. Return 3. ✓

Now try target = 6 (not present):

Step 1: lo=0, hi=6, mid=3. arr[3]=7 > 6 → target is to the LEFT → hi = mid-1 = 2 arr: 1 3 5 [7 9 11 13] ← eliminated [lo hi] Step 2: lo=0, hi=2, mid=1. arr[1]=3 < 6 → target is to the RIGHT → lo = mid+1 = 2 arr: 1 [3 5] ← eliminated right after [lo=hi=2] Step 3: lo=2, hi=2, mid=2. arr[2]=5 < 6 → lo = mid+1 = 3 lo=3 > hi=2 → interval empty → return -1

Notice: each step eliminates exactly the half that cannot contain the target. The loop terminates because lo strictly increases or hi strictly decreases at every step — the interval always shrinks.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
PSEUDOCODE lo = 0, hi = n - 1 while lo <= hi: mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 // safe midpoint (no overflow) if arr[mid] == target: return mid if arr[mid] < target: lo = mid + 1 // target in right half else: hi = mid - 1 // target in left half return -1 // lo > hi: interval exhausted
/* Return index of target in sorted array arr[0..n-1], or -1 if not found. */
int binary_search(int *arr, int n, int target) {
    int lo = 0;
    int hi = n - 1;

    while (lo <= hi) {
        /* Safe midpoint: (lo + hi) / 2 overflows when lo and hi are both near INT_MAX.
           lo + (hi - lo) / 2 is equivalent but hi-lo is always in range [0, n-1]. */
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;

        if (arr[mid] == target)
            return mid;           /* exact match */
        else if (arr[mid] < target)
            lo = mid + 1;         /* target must be in arr[mid+1..hi] */
        else
            hi = mid - 1;         /* target must be in arr[lo..mid-1] */
    }

    /* lo > hi: the live interval is empty; target is not in the array. */
    return -1;
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [2, 5, 8, 12, 16, 23, 38], n=7, target=23 iter | lo | hi | mid | arr[mid] | action -----|----|----|-----|----------|------- 1 | 0 | 6 | 3 | 12 | 12 < 23 → lo = 4 2 | 4 | 6 | 5 | 23 | match → return 5 ✓ target=10 (not present): iter | lo | hi | mid | arr[mid] | action -----|----|----|-----|----------|------- 1 | 0 | 6 | 3 | 12 | 12 > 10 → hi = 2 2 | 0 | 2 | 1 | 5 | 5 < 10 → lo = 2 3 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 8 | 8 < 10 → lo = 3 lo=3 > hi=2 → return -1 ✓
⚠ Gotcha: writing mid = (lo + hi) / 2

Wrong: int mid = (lo + hi) / 2; — if lo = 2,000,000,000 and hi = 2,000,000,001, their sum overflows a 32-bit int, producing a negative value.

Correct: int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2; — hi - lo is at most n-1, which always fits in a 32-bit int for any realistic array size.

One-line rule: always write mid as lo + (hi - lo) / 2, not (lo + hi) / 2.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. Why is the loop condition lo <= hi (not lo < hi)? What single-element array case would fail with strict less-than?
  2. When arr[mid] < target, why do we set lo = mid + 1 instead of lo = mid? What would go wrong if we forgot the +1?
  3. After the loop ends with return -1, what value does lo hold — and why is that useful for "Search Insert Position" (LeetCode 35)?
When you see: sorted array + find a specific target value → binary search: lo/hi converge, mid = lo + (hi−lo)/2, return −1 when lo > hi
  • Target not in array; find insertion point → same code; return lo after the loop (lo is where target would go)
  • Find first/last occurrence → don't return on match; keep searching (P15/P16)
  • Search on a comparator (strings, structs) → same skeleton; replace </> with cmp() return value
Pattern 15 Binary Search: Find Leftmost (First) Occurrence
① Background

The classic binary search from P14 returns any occurrence of the target — whichever one the midpoint happens to land on. But many real problems need the first or last occurrence, not just any. Counting how many times a value appears, finding the start of a range, or determining the insertion boundary all require knowing the exact leftmost (or rightmost) position.

The modification to classic binary search is minimal but conceptually important: when you find arr[mid] == target, you do not immediately return. Instead, you record mid as your best candidate so far and continue searching to the left by setting hi = mid - 1. You are saying "I found a match at mid, but there might be an earlier one in [lo, mid-1]." The loop eventually terminates when lo > hi, and the last recorded value of result is the leftmost occurrence.

This pattern also manifests as "find the first True in a boolean sequence." Imagine the array is transformed: each element is True if arr[i] == target and False otherwise. The array looks like [F, F, T, T, T, F] — first True at index 2. Finding the first True is exactly find-first-occurrence. This abstraction is the basis of the "answer space binary search" in P19.

In practice: database lower_bound() and upper_bound() are exactly find-first and find-last; C++ std::lower_bound returns an iterator to the first element not less than target; std::upper_bound returns the first element greater than target. Understanding P15/P16 makes those standard library functions immediately intuitive.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 34 — Find First and Last Position — textbook: find-first for left boundary, find-last for right boundary.
  • LeetCode 278 — First Bad Version — find the leftmost True in a boolean sequence (is_bad(v)); identical pattern.
  • C++ std::lower_bound: first element ≥ value in sorted range — this IS find-first; the standard library uses the same binary search skeleton.
  • Database range query: how many times does a value appear? — count = find_last(v) - find_first(v) + 1; O(log n) instead of O(n) scan.
  • Firmware event log: first occurrence of a fault code in a timestamped sorted log — find when a fault first appeared without scanning from the beginning.
  • Version bisection (git bisect): first commit where a test fails — commits are sorted by time; find the first "bad" commit is structurally identical to find-first.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [1, 2, 2, 2, 3, 4], target = 2. Three 2s sit at indices 1, 2, 3. We want index 1.

Classic binary search would hit index 2 (the midpoint) and return immediately. That is wrong. Instead:

lo=0, hi=5, result=-1 Step 1: mid = 0+(5-0)/2 = 2. arr[2] = 2 == target! → record result = 2 → set hi = mid-1 = 1 (search LEFT for an earlier match) arr: 1 [2] 2 2 3 4 ↑ ← still to check: [lo=0..hi=1] Step 2: lo=0, hi=1, mid = 0+(1-0)/2 = 0. arr[0] = 1 < 2 → lo = mid+1 = 1 (target is to the right) Step 3: lo=1, hi=1, mid=1. arr[1] = 2 == target! → record result = 1 (better candidate!) → set hi = mid-1 = 0 (search left again) Step 4: lo=1 > hi=0 → loop exits. return result = 1 ✓

The key difference from classic binary search: on a match, instead of returning, we record the index and keep the search going to the LEFT by setting hi = mid - 1. The last recorded value survives as the answer.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
PSEUDOCODE lo = 0, hi = n-1, result = -1 while lo <= hi: mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 if arr[mid] == target: result = mid // record candidate hi = mid - 1 // keep searching LEFT for earlier occurrence elif arr[mid] < target: lo = mid + 1 else: hi = mid - 1 return result
/* Return index of first (leftmost) occurrence of target, or -1 if not found. */
int find_first(int *arr, int n, int target) {
    int lo = 0, hi = n - 1;
    int result = -1;

    while (lo <= hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;

        if (arr[mid] == target) {
            result = mid;   /* found a match; record it as best candidate so far */
            hi = mid - 1;   /* but keep searching left — there might be an earlier one */
        } else if (arr[mid] < target) {
            lo = mid + 1;   /* target is in the right half */
        } else {
            hi = mid - 1;   /* target is in the left half */
        }
    }
    return result;  /* -1 if target was never found */
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [1, 2, 2, 2, 3, 4], n=6, target=2 iter | lo | hi | mid | arr[mid] | action | result -----|----|----|-----|----------|------------------|------- 1 | 0 | 5 | 2 | 2 | match→ hi=1 | 2 2 | 0 | 1 | 0 | 1 | 1<2 → lo=1 | 2 3 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 2 | match→ hi=0 | 1 lo=1 > hi=0 → return 1 ✓ target = 5 (not present): iter | lo | hi | mid | arr[mid] | action -----|----|----|-----|----------|------- 1 | 0 | 5 | 2 | 2 | 2 < 5 → lo=3 2 | 3 | 5 | 4 | 3 | 3 < 5 → lo=5 3 | 5 | 5 | 5 | 4 | 4 < 5 → lo=6 lo=6 > hi=5 → return -1 ✓
⚠ Gotcha: returning mid immediately on a match (classic binary search mistake)

Wrong: if (arr[mid] == target) return mid; — returns some occurrence (not necessarily the first), depending on where the midpoint happened to land.

Correct: record in result and set hi = mid - 1 to continue searching left. The last recorded value is the leftmost occurrence.

One-line rule: find-first = on match, save & go left (hi = mid - 1); find-last = on match, save & go right (lo = mid + 1).

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. Why does setting hi = mid - 1 on a match not risk skipping the true first occurrence?
  2. Using find_first and find_last, write the formula to count how many times a value appears in a sorted array in O(log n).
  3. LeetCode 278 (First Bad Version) asks for the first "bad" version. Describe in one sentence how to map that problem onto find_first without any array.
When you see: first occurrence / leftmost index of a value in sorted data → binary search, on match save result and set hi = mid − 1
  • Count occurrences of a value → find_last(v) − find_first(v) + 1
  • First version/index where condition becomes true → same pattern, condition replaces ==
  • C++ lower_bound equivalent → this is exactly what it does internally
Pattern 16 Binary Search: Find Rightmost (Last) Occurrence
① Background

Find-last is the exact mirror of find-first (P15). When you encounter arr[mid] == target, you record mid as your best candidate and continue searching to the right by setting lo = mid + 1. You are saying "I found a match at mid, but there might be a later one in [mid+1, hi]." The loop terminates when lo > hi, and result holds the rightmost index.

Together, find-first and find-last give you the complete range [first, last] of a value's occurrences in O(log n). This range has exactly last - first + 1 elements. The standard library equivalent in C++ is the pair returned by std::equal_range — a lower_bound (find-first) and an upper_bound (first element greater than target, which is find-last + 1). Knowing this, you can mentally translate any equal_range call into the two binary searches that compose it.

The most common mistake is writing a single "find-first-or-last" function that uses a boolean parameter to select direction. While technically correct, it is error-prone. Keep them as two clean functions. The code difference between them is exactly one line: after arr[mid] == target, either hi = mid - 1 (go left for first) or lo = mid + 1 (go right for last). Remembering that single difference is all you need.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 34 — Find First and Last Position (right boundary) — the second half of the answer; pair with find_first for the full range.
  • C++ std::upper_bound minus one — upper_bound returns the first element greater than target; subtract one to get the last occurrence.
  • Count occurrences of a value in a sorted array — find_last - find_first + 1 in O(log n) instead of scanning O(n).
  • Database: find the end of a range scan for a key — B-tree range scans need both the first and last leaf entry for a given key; this is the right boundary.
  • Firmware: latest timestamp with a given event code in a sorted event log — find the most recent occurrence without scanning all events from that code forward.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [1, 2, 2, 2, 3, 4], target = 2. Target is at indices 1, 2, 3. We want index 3.

lo=0, hi=5, result=-1 Step 1: mid=2. arr[2]=2 == target. → record result=2 → lo = mid+1 = 3 (search RIGHT for a later match) still to check: [lo=3..hi=5] Step 2: lo=3, hi=5, mid=4. arr[4]=3 > 2 → hi=3. Step 3: lo=3, hi=3, mid=3. arr[3]=2 == target. → record result=3 (better! more to the right) → lo = mid+1 = 4 Step 4: lo=4 > hi=3 → loop exits. return result = 3 ✓

The only structural difference from find-first: on a match, set lo = mid + 1 (go right) instead of hi = mid - 1 (go left). Everything else is identical.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Return index of last (rightmost) occurrence of target, or -1 if not found. */
int find_last(int *arr, int n, int target) {
    int lo = 0, hi = n - 1;
    int result = -1;

    while (lo <= hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;

        if (arr[mid] == target) {
            result = mid;   /* record this match as best candidate so far */
            lo = mid + 1;   /* keep searching RIGHT — there might be a later one */
        } else if (arr[mid] < target) {
            lo = mid + 1;
        } else {
            hi = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return result;
}

/* Count occurrences of target in sorted array arr[0..n-1]. */
int count_occurrences(int *arr, int n, int target) {
    int first = find_first(arr, n, target);
    if (first == -1) return 0;              /* not in array */
    int last  = find_last(arr, n, target);
    return last - first + 1;               /* range is [first, last] inclusive */
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [1, 2, 2, 2, 3, 4], n=6, target=2 iter | lo | hi | mid | arr[mid] | action | result -----|----|----|-----|----------|------------------|------- 1 | 0 | 5 | 2 | 2 | match→ lo=3 | 2 2 | 3 | 5 | 4 | 3 | 3>2 → hi=3 | 2 3 | 3 | 3 | 3 | 2 | match→ lo=4 | 3 lo=4 > hi=3 → return 3 ✓ count_occurrences(arr, 6, 2): find_first returns 1, find_last returns 3 count = 3 - 1 + 1 = 3 ✓
⚠ Gotcha: mixing up the direction after a match

Wrong for find-last: hi = mid - 1 after a match — this searches left and finds the first occurrence, not the last.

Correct for find-last: lo = mid + 1 — searches right for a later occurrence.

One-line rule: find-first → on match go left (hi = mid−1); find-last → on match go right (lo = mid+1). One line is all that differs.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. What does find_last return when the target does not appear in the array at all?
  2. If you use find_first and find_last together to count occurrences, what is the edge case you must handle before computing last - first + 1?
  3. How does std::upper_bound in C++ relate to find_last? If upper_bound returns an iterator to the first element greater than target, how would you use it to get the last equal element?
When you see: last occurrence / rightmost index / upper boundary of a value → binary search, on match save result and set lo = mid + 1
  • Count occurrences → find_last(v) − find_first(v) + 1 (check for -1 first)
  • Largest value ≤ target in sorted array → find_last of target or, if not found, the element just before where it would be inserted
  • C++ upper_bound → find_last + 1 position (first element strictly greater)
Pattern 17 Binary Search: Search in Rotated Sorted Array
① Background

A sorted array that has been "rotated" — cut at some pivot and the two halves swapped — is no longer globally sorted, so classic binary search fails. For example [1,2,3,4,5,6,7] rotated at pivot 4 becomes [4,5,6,7,1,2,3]. You cannot simply compare arr[mid] with target and decide which half to search, because arr[mid] might be in the "high" portion or the "low" portion of the rotation.

The critical observation is that even in a rotated array, at every possible midpoint exactly one of the two halves [lo..mid] or [mid..hi] is a clean sorted segment. The pivot, by definition, can only be in one of them. You can detect which half is sorted by comparing arr[lo] with arr[mid]: if arr[lo] ≤ arr[mid], the left half is sorted (the pivot is somewhere to the right of mid); otherwise the right half is sorted.

Once you know which half is sorted, you know its exact range of values: [arr[lo], arr[mid]] for the left half, or [arr[mid], arr[hi]] for the right half. You can then test in O(1) whether target falls within that range. If it does, discard the other half (standard binary search). If it does not, discard this sorted half and recurse into the unsorted one where the target must be.

This pattern is common in systems that maintain sorted ring buffers or circular logs — the most recently written block may be at any position, creating a logical rotation. Searching for a log entry by sequence number in such a structure uses exactly this algorithm.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 33 — Search in Rotated Sorted Array — textbook application; no duplicates version.
  • LeetCode 81 — Search in Rotated Sorted Array II — allows duplicates; the arr[lo] == arr[mid] case requires incrementing lo before deciding.
  • LeetCode 153 — Find Minimum in Rotated Sorted Array — the pivot point is the minimum; find the boundary between the high segment and the low segment.
  • Circular log search: find a log entry in a ring buffer sorted by sequence number — ring buffers are physically rotated arrays; binary search on sequence number uses this pattern.
  • Embedded OS: search a circular task priority queue by priority value — real-time systems sometimes store sorted priority arrays with circular wrap-around.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [4,5,6,7,0,1,2], target = 0. The array was originally [0,1,2,3,4,5,6,7] rotated at position 4.

At lo=0, hi=6: mid=3. arr[3]=7. Is the left half [4,5,6,7] sorted? Compare arr[lo]=4 with arr[mid]=7: 4 ≤ 7, yes, left is sorted. The left half spans values [4..7]. Is target=0 in that range? No (0 < 4). So target is NOT in the sorted left half — it must be in the right half.

arr: 4 5 6 7 0 1 2 idx: 0 1 2 3 4 5 6 [lo mid hi] arr[lo]=4 <= arr[mid]=7 → left half [4..7] is sorted target=0 NOT in [4, 7] → search RIGHT: lo = mid+1 = 4

Now lo=4, hi=6, mid=5. arr[5]=1. arr[lo]=0 ≤ arr[mid]=1, so left half [0,1] is sorted. Target=0 IS in [0, 1]. Search left: hi=4.

arr: 0 1 2 idx: 4 5 6 [lo mid hi] arr[lo]=0 <= arr[mid]=1 → left half [0,1] is sorted target=0 IS in [0, 1) → search LEFT: hi = mid-1 = 4 lo=4, hi=4, mid=4. arr[4]=0 == target → return 4 ✓

The logic: identify the sorted half, check if target is in that half's value range, and pick that half if yes, the other half if no. The sorted half is predictable; the unsorted half is where the pivot is hiding.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Search for target in a rotated sorted array (no duplicates).
   Returns index of target, or -1 if not found. */
int search_rotated(int *arr, int n, int target) {
    int lo = 0, hi = n - 1;

    while (lo <= hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;

        if (arr[mid] == target) return mid;  /* found */

        /* Determine which half is the cleanly sorted segment. */
        if (arr[lo] <= arr[mid]) {
            /* Left half [lo..mid] is sorted (no rotation in this segment).
               Its values span [arr[lo], arr[mid]]. */
            if (arr[lo] <= target && target < arr[mid]) {
                hi = mid - 1;   /* target is in the sorted left half */
            } else {
                lo = mid + 1;   /* target is in the (possibly unsorted) right half */
            }
        } else {
            /* Right half [mid..hi] is sorted (the rotation is in the left half).
               Its values span [arr[mid], arr[hi]]. */
            if (arr[mid] < target && target <= arr[hi]) {
                lo = mid + 1;   /* target is in the sorted right half */
            } else {
                hi = mid - 1;   /* target is in the (possibly unsorted) left half */
            }
        }
    }
    return -1;
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [4,5,6,7,0,1,2], n=7, target=0 iter | lo | hi | mid | arr[mid] | sorted half? | target in it? | action -----|----|----|-----|----------|--------------|---------------|------- 1 | 0 | 6 | 3 | 7 | left [4..7] | 0 not in [4,7) | lo=4 2 | 4 | 6 | 5 | 1 | left [0..1] | 0 in [0,1) | hi=4 3 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 0 | match | | return 4 ✓ target=3 (not present): iter | lo | hi | mid | arr[mid] | action -----|----|----|-----|----------|------- 1 | 0 | 6 | 3 | 7 | left [4..7] sorted, 3 not in [4,7) → lo=4 2 | 4 | 6 | 5 | 1 | left [0..1] sorted, 3 not in [0,1) → hi=4 3 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 0 | left [0..0] sorted, 3 not in [0,0) → lo=5 lo=5 > hi=4 → return -1 ✓
⚠ Gotcha: using strict < instead of ≤ when checking arr[lo] vs arr[mid]

Wrong: if (arr[lo] < arr[mid]) — when arr[lo] == arr[mid] (a one-element or two-element left half), this incorrectly falls into the right-half-is-sorted branch.

Correct: if (arr[lo] <= arr[mid]) — equal means the left half is trivially sorted (one element is always sorted).

One-line rule: use ≤ to detect which half is sorted; the boundary case (lo == mid) must be treated as "left is sorted."

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. If arr[lo] ≤ arr[mid], you know the left half is sorted. Why does that imply the rotation pivot is somewhere in the right half?
  2. What changes in the algorithm when the array may contain duplicates (LeetCode 81)? Which specific case becomes ambiguous?
  3. How would you adapt this algorithm to find the minimum element in a rotated sorted array (LeetCode 153) instead of a specific target?
When you see: rotated sorted array + find target → identify the sorted half (arr[lo] ≤ arr[mid]?), check if target is in its range, discard the other half
  • Find minimum in rotated array → find the pivot where the "drop" happens; arr[mid] > arr[hi] means pivot is in right half
  • With duplicates → when arr[lo] == arr[mid], increment lo by 1 (can't determine sorted side) and retry
  • Rotated array + find first occurrence of target → combine this rotation logic with the save-and-continue from P15
Pattern 18 Binary Search: Find Peak Element
① Background

In classic binary search, you compare arr[mid] against a known target. But what if there is no target — you just want to find any local maximum (a peak where both neighbors are smaller)? A linear scan finds a peak in O(n), but binary search can do it in O(log n) by using slope direction instead of value comparison.

The insight is geometric: if arr[mid] < arr[mid+1], the array is going "uphill" to the right of mid. A peak must exist somewhere to the right — either at mid+1, or further right if it keeps going up. We can safely discard [lo..mid] and restrict the search to [mid+1..hi]. Conversely, if arr[mid] > arr[mid+1], we are going "downhill" to the right; a peak exists at mid itself or somewhere to the left. We restrict to [lo..mid].

Notice the loop condition changes from lo <= hi (classic) to lo < hi (strict). This is because when we set hi = mid (not mid-1), we need the interval to strictly shrink — if lo == hi == mid, setting hi = mid would create an infinite loop. With strict lo < hi, the loop terminates when lo and hi converge to the same index, which is the peak.

This same "follow the uphill slope" logic applies to finding the maximum of a unimodal function (ternary search), finding the peak in a mountain array, and finding the minimum in a V-shaped sorted array. It is a general template for searching on a structure where "which direction to go" can be determined from local gradient information.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 162 — Find Peak Element — textbook application; any local maximum qualifies.
  • LeetCode 852 — Peak Index in a Mountain Array — the array has exactly one peak; same algorithm.
  • LeetCode 1095 — Find in Mountain Array — first find the peak, then binary-search the two sorted halves.
  • Signal processing: find the dominant frequency peak in a spectrum — power spectrum arrays often have a single dominant peak; binary search on slope finds it without scanning.
  • Optimization: find the maximum of a unimodal function sampled at discrete points — gradient-based binary search (ternary search) applies the same slope-direction logic.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [1, 3, 5, 3, 1]. The peak is at index 2 (value 5). Edges are treated as -∞, so index 0 and index 4 cannot be peaks unless they are greater than their one neighbor.

Key question at each step: is the slope from mid going UP or DOWN to the right?

arr: 1 3 5 3 1 idx: 0 1 2 3 4 lo=0, hi=4: mid=2. arr[2]=5, arr[3]=3. 5 > 3 → downhill right → peak at mid or left. Set hi = mid = 2. lo=0, hi=2: mid=1. arr[1]=3, arr[2]=5. 3 < 5 → uphill right → peak to the right. Set lo = mid+1 = 2. lo=2, hi=2: lo == hi → loop exits. Return 2. ✓

Notice hi = mid (not mid-1) when going downhill. We keep mid in the search range because mid itself might be the peak. Using hi = mid-1 would incorrectly exclude the peak in the case where arr[mid] IS the peak.

Also notice the loop is while (lo < hi), not while (lo <= hi). With while (lo <= hi) and hi = mid, if lo == hi == mid, we'd set hi = mid = lo and loop forever. The strict condition ensures lo and hi always converge.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Find any peak element: arr[i] > arr[i-1] and arr[i] > arr[i+1].
   Edges treated as -infinity (so arr[0] and arr[n-1] count if they beat their one neighbor).
   Guaranteed: arr has no two adjacent equal elements (standard problem constraint). */
int find_peak(int *arr, int n) {
    int lo = 0, hi = n - 1;

    /* Strict lo < hi (not lo <= hi) because hi = mid (not mid-1) prevents
       the loop from terminating when lo == hi == mid with lo <= hi. */
    while (lo < hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;

        if (arr[mid] < arr[mid + 1]) {
            /* Uphill to the right: arr[mid] cannot be a peak.
               A peak must exist in [mid+1, hi]. */
            lo = mid + 1;
        } else {
            /* Downhill to the right (arr[mid] > arr[mid+1]):
               arr[mid] is a candidate peak (or the peak is to the left).
               Keep mid in range: hi = mid (not mid-1). */
            hi = mid;
        }
    }

    /* lo == hi: converged on the peak. */
    return lo;
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [1, 2, 3, 1], n=4 iter | lo | hi | mid | arr[mid] | arr[mid+1] | slope | action -----|----|----|-----|----------|------------|----------|------- 1 | 0 | 3 | 1 | 2 | 3 | uphill | lo=2 2 | 2 | 3 | 2 | 3 | 1 | downhill | hi=2 lo=2 == hi=2 → return 2. arr[2]=3 is a peak ✓ arr = [3, 2, 1], n=3 (peak at index 0) iter | lo | hi | mid | arr[mid] | arr[mid+1] | slope | action -----|----|----|-----|----------|------------|----------|------- 1 | 0 | 2 | 1 | 2 | 1 | downhill | hi=1 2 | 0 | 1 | 0 | 3 | 2 | downhill | hi=0 lo=0 == hi=0 → return 0. arr[0]=3 is a peak ✓
⚠ Gotcha: using while(lo <= hi) with hi = mid

Wrong: while (lo <= hi) { ... hi = mid; } — when lo == hi == mid, setting hi = mid leaves the interval unchanged and the loop runs forever (infinite loop).

Correct: while (lo < hi) { ... hi = mid; } — strict less-than guarantees lo < hi at loop entry, so mid < hi, so setting hi = mid strictly shrinks the interval.

One-line rule: whenever you use hi = mid (not mid−1), you MUST use while(lo < hi) or you will infinite-loop.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. When arr[mid] < arr[mid+1], why is it guaranteed that a peak exists in [mid+1, hi] and not just "might exist"?
  2. If you used hi = mid - 1 instead of hi = mid in the downhill case, what specific input would cause you to miss a valid peak?
  3. This algorithm finds any peak. How would you modify it to find the global maximum instead of any local maximum?
When you see: find any local maximum (peak) with no specific target value → binary search on slope: uphill right → lo=mid+1; downhill right → hi=mid; use while(lo < hi)
  • Exactly one peak (mountain array) → same algorithm; answer is unique
  • Find in mountain array → find peak first, then binary-search each sorted half
  • Maximum of a unimodal function → ternary search is the continuous equivalent of this slope-based approach
Pattern 19 Binary Search: Answer Space (Minimize/Maximize a Value)
① Background

All previous binary search patterns searched for a specific value within a sorted array. This pattern is different: you binary search the space of possible answers rather than the array. The problem statement asks for the minimum (or maximum) value that satisfies some constraint — "what is the smallest ship capacity that delivers all packages within D days?" — and the key property that makes binary search applicable is that feasibility is monotone: if capacity X works, then capacity X+1 also works.

Monotone feasibility means the answer space looks like [impossible, impossible, ..., possible, possible, possible]. You want the first "possible" (minimization) or the last "possible" (maximization). Binary search on the answer space finds this boundary in O(log(hi - lo)) calls to the feasibility check. If the feasibility check itself runs in O(n), the total time is O(n log(hi-lo)), which is typically O(n log n) or O(n log MAX_VALUE).

The hardest part of applying this pattern is recognizing that it is applicable. Ask: "if answer A is valid, is answer A+1 also valid?" If yes, the feasibility is monotone and you can binary search. Common signals: "minimum X such that...", "maximum X such that...", "find the smallest/largest value where a condition holds." Another signal: the constraint has a parameter that, when made larger, always helps and never hurts (or vice versa).

This pattern appears everywhere in systems engineering: minimum bandwidth to stream video at required quality, minimum number of servers to handle peak load, minimum allocation size to hold a dataset, maximum batch size that fits in GPU memory. Any time you are tuning a single numeric parameter and have a way to check whether a given parameter value "works," binary search on answer space is applicable.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 875 — Koko Eating Bananas — minimize eating speed: if speed k works, k+1 also works → binary search k.
  • LeetCode 1011 — Capacity To Ship Packages Within D Days — minimize ship capacity: same monotone structure.
  • LeetCode 410 — Split Array Largest Sum — minimize the maximum subarray sum when split into k parts; feasibility check is O(n).
  • Systems: minimum memory allocation to run a workload without swapping — feasibility = workload fits; larger allocation always feasible → binary search allocation size.
  • Networking: minimum bandwidth to achieve required quality of service — more bandwidth always helps; find the minimum that meets the SLA.
  • Database: minimum block size for a B-tree to avoid splits on a given workload — larger block sizes reduce splits monotonically; binary search on block size.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Example: piles = [3, 6, 7, 11], h = 8 (Koko eats bananas). What is the minimum eating speed k (bananas/hour) to finish all piles in h = 8 hours?

The answer range: lo = 1 (must eat at least 1/hour), hi = max(piles) = 11 (eating the biggest pile in one hour is always enough). The feasibility check: can Koko eat all piles at speed k in ≤ h hours?

Feasibility for k=6: ceil(3/6)+ceil(6/6)+ceil(7/6)+ceil(11/6) = 1+1+2+2 = 6 ≤ 8 ✓ Feasibility for k=3: ceil(3/3)+ceil(6/3)+ceil(7/3)+ceil(11/3) = 1+2+3+4 = 10 > 8 ✗ Feasibility for k=4: 1+2+2+3 = 8 ≤ 8 ✓ Feasibility for k=1: 3+6+7+11 = 27 > 8 ✗ Answer space: k=1(✗) k=2(✗) k=3(✗) k=4(✓) k=5(✓) k=6(✓) ... k=11(✓) ↑ first feasible = answer

The answer space is a sorted boolean array: all ✗ on the left, all ✓ on the right. Finding the first ✓ is exactly find-first (P15) on this boolean array. You binary search the range [1..11] and call feasible(mid) at each step.

Binary search trace: lo=1, hi=11. mid=6→feasible, record 6, hi=5. mid=3→not feasible, lo=4. mid=4→feasible, record 4, hi=3. lo=4 > hi=3 → return 4.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
PSEUDOCODE — minimize: find smallest answer in [lo..hi] where feasible()=true result = hi (worst case: maximum always feasible) while lo <= hi: mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 if feasible(mid): result = mid // record; try smaller hi = mid - 1 else: lo = mid + 1 // too small; try larger return result
/* Check: can we eat all piles at speed k within h hours? */
static int can_eat(int *piles, int n, int k, int h) {
    int hours = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        /* Integer ceiling division: ceil(a/b) = (a + b - 1) / b */
        hours += (piles[i] + k - 1) / k;
    return hours <= h;
}

/* Minimum eating speed to finish all piles within h hours. */
int min_eating_speed(int *piles, int n, int h) {
    int lo = 1;
    int hi = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (piles[i] > hi) hi = piles[i];   /* max pile size = maximum possible speed */

    int result = hi;   /* worst case: eat the biggest pile in one hour, always works */

    while (lo <= hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;

        if (can_eat(piles, n, mid, h)) {
            /* Speed mid works. Record it and try a SLOWER speed (minimize). */
            result = mid;
            hi = mid - 1;
        } else {
            /* Speed mid is too slow. Need to go FASTER. */
            lo = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    return result;
}
⑤ Dry Run
piles=[3,6,7,11], h=8, lo=1, hi=11 iter | lo | hi | mid | can_eat? | action | result -----|----|----|-----|----------|---------------|------- 1 | 1 | 11 | 6 | YES | hi=5, save 6 | 6 2 | 1 | 5 | 3 | NO | lo=4 | 6 3 | 4 | 5 | 4 | YES | hi=3, save 4 | 4 lo=4 > hi=3 → return 4 ✓ can_eat(4): ceil(3/4)+ceil(6/4)+ceil(7/4)+ceil(11/4) = 1+2+2+3 = 8 ≤ 8 ✓ can_eat(3): ceil(3/3)+ceil(6/3)+ceil(7/3)+ceil(11/3) = 1+2+3+4 = 10 > 8 ✗
⚠ Gotcha: searching in the wrong direction after a feasible result

Wrong (for minimize): if (feasible) lo = mid + 1; — searches right after finding a feasible answer, finding the largest feasible value instead of the smallest.

Correct (for minimize): if (feasible) { result = mid; hi = mid - 1; } — records the candidate and searches left for a smaller valid answer.

One-line rule: minimize → feasible means "this works, but try smaller" (hi = mid-1); maximize → feasible means "this works, but try larger" (lo = mid+1).

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. Before applying this pattern, what property of the feasibility function must you verify? Give a counterexample where binary search on answer space gives a wrong answer.
  2. For a maximization problem (find the largest valid answer), what changes in the binary search template compared to the minimization version?
  3. Why is integer ceiling division written as (a + b - 1) / b instead of using ceil((double)a / b), and when would the floating-point version silently give the wrong answer?
When you see: minimize/maximize a numeric answer + "if X works, X+1 also works" → binary search the answer space [lo..hi] with a feasibility check
  • Minimize: on feasible record & search left (hi = mid−1)
  • Maximize: on feasible record & search right (lo = mid+1)
  • Feasibility check runs in O(n) → total O(n log(hi−lo))
Pattern 20 Kadane’s Algorithm: Maximum Subarray Sum
① Background

Maximum subarray sum was first posed as a computational geometry problem in 1977. The naive approach tries all O(n²) pairs and computes each sum in O(n): O(n³) total. A prefix-sum trick reduces sum computation to O(1), giving O(n²). Kadane's algorithm, published in 1984, achieves O(n) with a single pass — one of the most elegant DP-to-greedy reductions in computer science.

The recurrence is simple: define current(i) as the maximum sum of any subarray ending at index i. There are only two choices for what that subarray looks like: either it starts fresh at index i (the single-element subarray [arr[i]]), or it extends the best subarray ending at i-1 (current(i-1) + arr[i]). Whichever is larger is current(i). The global answer is the maximum of current(i) across all i.

The greedy interpretation is even simpler: if your running sum is negative, it is a liability — adding it to any future element makes that element's contribution smaller. Better to start fresh. If your running sum is positive, extending it can only help. So: extend if positive, restart if the extension would be worse than starting fresh.

The most common mistake is initialising current and max_sum to 0 instead of arr[0]. This works for arrays with at least one positive element, but fails for all-negative arrays like [-3, -1, -2] where the correct answer is -1 (you must pick at least one element). Initialising to arr[0] and starting the loop at i=1 handles this correctly.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 53 — Maximum Subarray — textbook Kadane's; the original problem.
  • LeetCode 918 — Maximum Sum Circular Subarray — max of: Kadane's on the linear array OR (total sum − minimum subarray sum found by Kadane's on negated array).
  • LeetCode 152 — Maximum Product Subarray — Kadane's variant tracking both max and min product (negatives can flip sign).
  • Finance: maximum gain over a contiguous trading period — daily returns are the array; max subarray sum = the best holding period's total return.
  • Signal processing: find the signal segment with highest energy — energy is sum of squared amplitudes; Kadane's on the energy array finds the peak-energy window.
  • Network diagnostics: longest run of positive net bandwidth gain — Kadane's on the delta-bandwidth array identifies the best consecutive measurement window.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [-2, 1, -3, 4, -1, 2, 1, -5, 4]. We want the maximum sum over any contiguous subarray.

At each position i, ask: "What is the maximum sum of any subarray ending exactly at index i?"

i=0: Only option is [-2] itself. current = -2, max = -2 i=1: Extend [-2,1] gives -1. Start fresh [1] gives 1. Max of {-1, 1} = 1. current = 1, max = 1 i=2: Extend [1,-3] gives -2. Start fresh [-3] gives -3. Max of {-2,-3} = -2. current = -2, max = 1 i=3: Extend [-2,4] gives 2. Start fresh [4] gives 4. Max of {2, 4} = 4. current = 4, max = 4 ← running sum was negative, restart was better! i=4: Extend [4,-1] gives 3. Start fresh [-1] gives -1. current = 3, max = 4 i=5: Extend [3,2] gives 5. current = 5, max = 5 i=6: Extend [5,1] gives 6. current = 6, max = 6 ← global best i=7: Extend [6,-5] gives 1. current = 1, max = 6 i=8: Extend [1,4] gives 5. current = 5, max = 6

At i=3, the running sum was -2 (negative), so starting fresh at 4 was better than extending. That is the restart rule: current becomes arr[i] when current + arr[i] < arr[i], i.e., when current is negative.

Plain-English: seed with arr[0]. For each subsequent element: if extending is better (current > 0), extend; else restart at this element. Track the global maximum separately.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
PSEUDOCODE current = arr[0] max_sum = arr[0] for i from 1 to n-1: current = max(arr[i], current + arr[i]) // if current was negative, current+arr[i] < arr[i], so we restart max_sum = max(max_sum, current) return max_sum
/* Maximum sum of any contiguous subarray of arr[0..n-1].
   At least one element must be included (handles all-negative arrays). */
int max_subarray(int *arr, int n) {
    int current = arr[0];  /* max sum of subarray ending at index 0 */
    int max_sum = arr[0];  /* global best seen so far */

    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        /* Decision: extend the previous subarray, or start a new one at arr[i]?
           current + arr[i] > arr[i]  ↔  current > 0  (the running sum was positive).
           When current is negative, extending it would only hurt arr[i]'s contribution. */
        if (current + arr[i] > arr[i])
            current = current + arr[i];   /* extend: previous sum was a net positive */
        else
            current = arr[i];             /* restart: drop the negative running sum */

        /* Update the global maximum regardless of whether we extended or restarted. */
        if (current > max_sum)
            max_sum = current;
    }
    return max_sum;
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [-2, 1, -3, 4, -1, 2, 1, -5, 4] i | arr[i] | current+arr[i] | arr[i] | current | max_sum ---|--------|----------------|--------|---------|-------- 0 | -2 | — | init | -2 | -2 1 | 1 | -2+1=-1 | 1 | 1 | 1 (restart: -1 < 1) 2 | -3 | 1-3=-2 | -3 | -2 | 1 (extend: -2 > -3) 3 | 4 | -2+4=2 | 4 | 4 | 4 (restart: 2 < 4) 4 | -1 | 4-1=3 | -1 | 3 | 4 (extend: 3 > -1) 5 | 2 | 3+2=5 | 2 | 5 | 5 (extend) 6 | 1 | 5+1=6 | 1 | 6 | 6 (extend) ← global max 7 | -5 | 6-5=1 | -5 | 1 | 6 (extend: 1 > -5) 8 | 4 | 1+4=5 | 4 | 5 | 6 (extend) return 6 ✓ (subarray [4,-1,2,1] at indices 3..6) All-negative case: arr=[-3,-1,-2] current=-3, max=-3 i=1: max(-1, -3-1=-4) = -1, current=-1, max=-1 i=2: max(-2, -1-2=-3) = -2, current=-2, max=-1 return -1 ✓ (not 0!)
⚠ Gotcha: initialising current and max_sum to 0

Wrong: int current = 0, max_sum = 0; — for arr = [-3, -1, -2], every element is negative so the loop never updates current above 0, and the function returns 0. But 0 corresponds to an empty subarray; the problem requires at least one element, so -1 is the correct answer.

Correct: int current = arr[0], max_sum = arr[0]; and start the loop at i=1. The first element is always the base case.

One-line rule: seed Kadane's with arr[0], not 0; start the loop at i=1.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. The condition current + arr[i] > arr[i] is equivalent to current > 0. Rewrite the extend/restart decision using just that simpler check — what does it look like?
  2. Kadane's finds the maximum sum but does not tell you which subarray it corresponds to. How would you modify the code to also track the start and end indices?
  3. For the circular maximum subarray (LeetCode 918), a subarray can wrap around the end of the array. What is the formula relating the circular maximum to the linear maximum and the minimum subarray sum?
When you see: maximum (or minimum) sum of any contiguous subarray → Kadane's: seed with arr[0], extend if current > 0, restart if current ≤ 0, track global max
  • Circular subarray → max(Kadane linear, total_sum − Kadane on negated array)
  • Maximum product subarray → track both max and min at each step (negatives flip sign)
  • Minimum subarray sum → negate all elements, run Kadane's, negate the result
Pattern 21 Kadane’s Variant: Maximum Product Subarray
① Background

Maximum sum subarray (P20) only needed one running value because sums are monotone: adding a positive number always increases the sum, adding a negative always decreases it. Products behave differently. A large negative product multiplied by another negative number becomes a large positive — so the worst running product at index i-1 might become the best at index i if arr[i] is negative.

The fix is to track two running values simultaneously: the maximum product of any subarray ending at the current index (hi), and the minimum product of any subarray ending at the current index (lo). At each step, the new hi is the maximum of three candidates: arr[i] alone (fresh start), hi × arr[i] (extend the current max), and lo × arr[i] (extend the current min, which might flip sign). The new lo is the minimum of those same three candidates.

The critical implementation detail: you must compute both new hi and new lo from the OLD hi and lo values. If you update hi first and then use it to compute lo, you get the wrong answer. The safe approach is to compute all three candidate values before assigning anything, using temporary variables to hold the old hi and lo.

This two-value tracking technique generalises to any problem where both a running maximum and running minimum matter at each step — for example, maximum subarray with at most one deletion, or maximum product with mixed-sign matrices.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 152 — Maximum Product Subarray — direct application; the two-variable Kadane is the standard O(n) solution.
  • Signal processing: maximum energy (product of amplitudes) in a contiguous burst — amplitudes can be negative (phase inversion); the min-tracking handles sign flips.
  • Finance: maximum multiplicative return over a contiguous period — returns are multiplicative; negative returns can flip to gains over two periods.
  • LeetCode 713 — Subarray Product Less Than K — sliding window variant; uses a running product instead of sum.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [2, 3, -2, 4]. Goal: maximum product of any contiguous subarray.

At each index, the subarray ending here can be: just arr[i], or arr[i] times the previous best (or worst). Compute all three candidates from saved old values, then pick hi = max and lo = min.

i=0: hi=2, lo=2, ans=2 i=1 (arr[1]=3): old_hi=2, old_lo=2 candidates: [3, 2*3=6, 2*3=6] hi = max(3,6,6) = 6 lo = min(3,6,6) = 3 ans = 6 i=2 (arr[2]=-2): old_hi=6, old_lo=3 candidates: [-2, 6*-2=-12, 3*-2=-6] hi = max(-2, -12, -6) = -2 lo = min(-2, -12, -6) = -12 ans = 6 (no improvement) i=3 (arr[3]=4): old_hi=-2, old_lo=-12 candidates: [4, -2*4=-8, -12*4=-48] hi = max(4, -8, -48) = 4 lo = min(4, -8, -48) = -48 ans = 6

At i=2, arr[2]=-2 flipped everything: the previous hi (6) became the most negative product (-12), stored in lo. If a future negative number appeared, -12 × negative would give a large positive. That is exactly why we keep lo around.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
PSEUDOCODE hi = lo = ans = arr[0] for i from 1 to n-1: save old_hi = hi, old_lo = lo hi = max(arr[i], old_hi * arr[i], old_lo * arr[i]) lo = min(arr[i], old_hi * arr[i], old_lo * arr[i]) ans = max(ans, hi) return ans
/* Maximum product of any contiguous subarray. */
int max_product(int *arr, int n) {
    int hi  = arr[0];  /* max product of any subarray ending at current index */
    int lo  = arr[0];  /* min product of any subarray ending at current index */
    int ans = arr[0];

    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        /* Save old values BEFORE updating — both hi and lo depend on the OLD hi and lo.
           Updating hi first and using new hi to compute lo gives the wrong answer. */
        int old_hi = hi, old_lo = lo;

        /* Three candidates: start fresh, extend old max, extend old min (sign may flip). */
        int a = arr[i];
        int b = old_hi * arr[i];
        int c = old_lo * arr[i];

        hi = a;
        if (b > hi) hi = b;
        if (c > hi) hi = c;

        lo = a;
        if (b < lo) lo = b;
        if (c < lo) lo = c;

        if (hi > ans) ans = hi;
    }
    return ans;
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [2, 3, -2, 4] i | arr[i] | old_hi | old_lo | a | b | c | hi | lo | ans --|--------|--------|--------|----|------|------|-----|-----|---- 0 | — | — | — | — | — | — | 2 | 2 | 2 1 | 3 | 2 | 2 | 3 | 6 | 6 | 6 | 3 | 6 2 | -2 | 6 | 3 |-2 | -12 | -6 | -2 | -12 | 6 3 | 4 | -2 | -12 | 4 | -8 | -48 | 4 | -48 | 6 Answer: 6 (subarray [2,3] at indices 0..1) ✓ arr = [-2, 3, -4]: i=1(3): a=3, b=-2*3=-6, c=-2*3=-6 → hi=3, lo=-6, ans=3 i=2(-4): a=-4, b=3*-4=-12, c=-6*-4=24 → hi=24, lo=-12, ans=24 ✓ ([-2,3,-4])
⚠ Gotcha: updating hi before computing lo

Wrong: hi = max(arr[i], hi*arr[i], lo*arr[i]); lo = min(arr[i], hi*arr[i], lo*arr[i]); — the second line uses the already-updated hi, so lo is computed from new-hi × arr[i], not old-hi × arr[i].

Correct: save old_hi = hi before any updates, then use old_hi in both the new hi and new lo computations.

One-line rule: compute all candidates from old values first, assign both hi and lo from those saved candidates.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. Why does multiplying the minimum product by a negative number potentially give the new maximum? Walk through a concrete example.
  2. The code tracks three candidates for each of hi and lo. Can you prove that these three are exhaustive — that no other candidate could yield a higher or lower product?
  3. What happens if the array contains a zero? How does the fresh-start candidate (arr[i] alone) handle the reset?
When you see: maximum product subarray (with possible negatives) → track both hi and lo at each step; save old values before updating both
  • Array contains zeros → the fresh-start candidate resets naturally; no special handling needed
  • Minimum product subarray → same code, track lo separately; return lo at end
  • Even number of negatives guarantees positive max → true, but the algorithm handles it without needing to count
Pattern 22 Kadane’s Variant: Circular Maximum Subarray
① Background

Linear Kadane's (P20) finds the maximum sum subarray within the array bounds. When the array is circular — the last element wraps around to connect back to the first — the maximum subarray might span that wrap point. For example in [5, -3, 5], the best subarray is [5, 5] which wraps around, with sum 10, better than the linear best of 7.

The key insight is a complement argument. A wrapping subarray is everything EXCEPT some contiguous middle segment. If the total array sum is T and the middle (non-wrapping) segment has sum M, the wrapping subarray has sum T − M. To maximise T − M, we minimise M. Minimising a contiguous subarray sum is exactly Kadane's algorithm run "in reverse" — track the running minimum instead of maximum.

So the answer is max(linear_max, total − min_subarray_sum). Run one Kadane's pass tracking both max and min simultaneously, then apply the formula. This is O(n) time and O(1) space.

One edge case destroys the formula: if all elements are negative, the minimum subarray equals the total, making total − min = 0 — but an empty subarray is invalid. Guard: if linear_max < 0 (all negative), return linear_max directly and skip the circular candidate.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 918 — Maximum Sum Circular Subarray — direct application of the complement formula.
  • Circular buffer processing: maximum energy segment in a ring buffer — sensor data stored in circular buffers; the peak-energy window may wrap around the write pointer.
  • Scheduling: maximum-value task sequence in a circular job queue — jobs wrap around; the complement trick applies directly.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [5, -3, 5], total = 7. Two cases for a circular subarray:

Case 1 — no wrap (standard Kadane's): Best subarray entirely within arr[0..2] Kadane's max: 5 → max(5, 5-3=2)=5 → max(5, 5+5=10)=10... wait Actually: i=0:cur=5,max=5. i=1:cur=max(-3,5-3=2)=2,max=5. i=2:cur=max(5,2+5=7)=7,max=7 Linear max = 7 (subarray [5,-3,5]) Case 2 — wraps around (complement trick): Wrapping subarray = total - (minimum middle segment) Kadane's MIN: i=0:cur=5,min=5. i=1:cur=min(-3,5-3=2)=-3,min=-3. i=2:cur=min(5,-3+5=2)=2,min=-3 min_sum = -3 (the segment [-3]) circular = total - min_sum = 7 - (-3) = 10 (wrapping subarray [5,5] = 10)

Answer = max(linear_max=7, circular=10) = 10. The wrapping [5,5] wins over the linear [5,-3,5].

Now the all-negative edge case: arr = [-1, -2, -3]. total = -6. linear_max = -1. min_sum = -6 (the whole array). circular = -6 - (-6) = 0. But 0 means empty subarray — invalid. The guard if (max_sum < 0) return max_sum short-circuits to return -1.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Maximum sum subarray in a circular array (wrapping allowed). */
int max_circular(int *arr, int n) {
    int total   = 0;
    /* Seed both Kadane's passes with arr[0]; start loop at i=0 to accumulate total. */
    int cur_max = arr[0], max_sum = arr[0];
    int cur_min = arr[0], min_sum = arr[0];

    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        total += arr[i];

        /* Kadane's for maximum: extend or restart. */
        cur_max = (cur_max + arr[i] > arr[i]) ? cur_max + arr[i] : arr[i];
        if (cur_max > max_sum) max_sum = cur_max;

        /* Kadane's for minimum: same logic, opposite comparison.
           This finds the contiguous middle segment to "remove" for the circular case. */
        cur_min = (cur_min + arr[i] < arr[i]) ? cur_min + arr[i] : arr[i];
        if (cur_min < min_sum) min_sum = cur_min;
    }

    /* Guard: if all elements are negative, total - min_sum = 0 (empty subarray), invalid.
       max_sum < 0 detects this: when all elements negative, the best is the least-negative. */
    if (max_sum < 0) return max_sum;

    /* Circular candidate: remove the minimum middle segment from the total. */
    int circular = total - min_sum;
    return max_sum > circular ? max_sum : circular;
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [5, -3, 5], total = 7 i=0: cur_max=5, max_sum=5; cur_min=5, min_sum=5 i=1: cur_max=max(-3, 5-3=2)=2, max_sum=5; cur_min=min(-3, 5-3=2)=-3, min_sum=-3 i=2: cur_max=max(5, 2+5=7)=7, max_sum=7; cur_min=min(5, -3+5=2)=2, min_sum=-3 max_sum=7 (not < 0, proceed) circular = 7 - (-3) = 10 return max(7, 10) = 10 ✓ All-negative: arr=[-1,-2,-3], total=-6 max_sum=-1 (least negative), min_sum=-6 max_sum < 0 → return -1 ✓ (skip circular=0)
⚠ Gotcha: forgetting the all-negative guard

Wrong: no guard — returns max(-1, -6-(-6)=0) = 0 for an all-negative array, claiming the empty subarray has sum 0, which is invalid when at least one element is required.

Correct: if (max_sum < 0) return max_sum; before computing the circular candidate. This detects all-negative arrays (where linear_max is the least-negative element).

One-line rule: if max_sum < 0, all elements are negative — skip the circular candidate and return max_sum.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. Why does "total − min_subarray_sum" give the sum of the wrapping subarray? Draw a picture with a 5-element array to convince yourself.
  2. What does the circular candidate equal when the minimum subarray is the entire array? Why is that case only reached when all elements are negative?
  3. How would you extend this algorithm to find the maximum product (not sum) in a circular array?
When you see: maximum subarray in a circular / wrap-around array → max(Kadane linear, total − Kadane minimum); guard against all-negative with max_sum < 0 check
  • Minimum circular subarray → min(Kadane_min, total − Kadane_max); guard total − max = 0
