  • K-concatenation max sum → for K≥2, answer is max(one-array max, K×total + circular adjustment)
  • The complement trick works for any "remove a contiguous piece to maximise what remains" problem
Pattern 23 In-Place: Cyclic Sort (1-to-N Values)
① Background

When an array contains integers in the range [1, n] and you need to find missing or duplicate values, the obvious approaches use O(n) extra space (a boolean seen[] array) or O(n log n) time (sort then scan). Cyclic sort achieves O(n) time and O(1) space by exploiting the fact that value v has exactly one correct home: index v−1.

The algorithm walks through the array. At each position i, if arr[i] does not equal i+1 (value is not in its correct home), swap arr[i] to its correct position arr[arr[i]−1]. Crucially, do not advance i after a swap — the newly arrived element might itself be out of place. Only advance when arr[i] == i+1, meaning position i is settled.

The total number of swaps is at most n−1, because each swap moves at least one element into its correct final position. An element already in the correct position is never swapped. This amortised argument gives O(n) overall even though there is a while loop inside the walk.

The duplicate-value guard is essential: if arr[i] == arr[arr[i]−1], both positions already hold the same value (a duplicate). Swapping them would produce an infinite loop because nothing changes. The guard condition arr[i] != arr[correct] detects this and advances i past the duplicate instead of swapping.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 41 — First Missing Positive — cyclic sort to place 1..n, then scan for first gap.
  • LeetCode 442 — Find All Duplicates in an Array — after cyclic sort, any index where arr[i] ≠ i+1 holds a duplicate.
  • LeetCode 268 — Missing Number — simpler variant: XOR or sum formula works, but cyclic sort also applies.
  • Embedded: validate that a 1-to-N sensor ID assignment has no gaps or repeats — hardware ID arrays must be a permutation; cyclic sort checks this in place without extra memory.
  • Inventory check: verify items numbered 1 to N are all present exactly once — cyclic sort reveals missing or duplicate item numbers in a single O(n) pass.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [3, 1, 4, 2, 5], n = 5. Value 3 belongs at index 2, value 1 at index 0, etc.

arr: 3 1 4 2 5 idx: 0 1 2 3 4 i=0: arr[0]=3. Correct home = index 2. arr[2]=4 ≠ 3, so swap idx 0 and 2. → arr: 4 1 3 2 5 (3 is now at its home, index 2 ✓) i=0: arr[0]=4. Correct home = index 3. arr[3]=2 ≠ 4, swap idx 0 and 3. → arr: 2 1 3 4 5 (4 is now at index 3 ✓) i=0: arr[0]=2. Correct home = index 1. arr[1]=1 ≠ 2, swap idx 0 and 1. → arr: 1 2 3 4 5 (2 is now at index 1 ✓) i=0: arr[0]=1 == 0+1 ✓. Advance. i=1,2,3,4 all correct. Done.

Three swaps placed all five elements. Each swap permanently fixed one element's position. We never revisit a settled index. That is the O(n) guarantee.

Duplicate case: arr = [3, 1, 3, 2, 4]. At i=0: arr[0]=3, correct=2. arr[2]=3 == arr[0]=3. The guard arr[i] != arr[correct] triggers: advance i without swapping. Position 0 will be identified as "wrong" in the final scan, revealing the duplicate 3.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Cyclic sort: place each value v at index v-1 (for values in range [1..n]). */
void cyclic_sort(int *arr, int n) {
    int i = 0;
    while (i < n) {
        int correct = arr[i] - 1;  /* value arr[i] belongs at this index */

        /* Guard: if arr[i] already equals arr[correct], either arr[i] is already
           at its correct position (arr[i] == i+1), OR it's a duplicate of whatever
           is already at index correct. Either way, swapping would loop forever. */
        if (arr[i] != arr[correct]) {
            int tmp      = arr[i];
            arr[i]       = arr[correct];
            arr[correct] = tmp;
            /* Don't advance i: newly arrived element may also be misplaced. */
        } else {
            i++;  /* position settled (correct or duplicate): move on */
        }
    }
}

/* Find the first missing positive in arr[0..n-1] using cyclic sort. */
int first_missing_positive(int *arr, int n) {
    cyclic_sort(arr, n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (arr[i] != i + 1) return i + 1;  /* value i+1 is absent */
    return n + 1;                            /* all 1..n present; missing is n+1 */
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [3, 1, 4, 2, 5] i=0: correct=2, arr[2]=4 ≠ arr[0]=3 → swap(0,2) → [4,1,3,2,5] i=0: correct=3, arr[3]=2 ≠ arr[0]=4 → swap(0,3) → [2,1,3,4,5] i=0: correct=1, arr[1]=1 ≠ arr[0]=2 → swap(0,1) → [1,2,3,4,5] i=0: arr[0]=1 == arr[0]=1 → i++ (correct=0, arr[0]=1 == i+1=1) i=1,2,3,4: all arr[i]==i+1 → advance each Scan: no gap → return 6 arr = [3, 4, -1, 1] (values out of 1..n range): Values -1 and out-of-range: correct = arr[i]-1 could be negative. Fix: skip values outside [1..n]: if arr[i] < 1 || arr[i] > n → just i++ (standard version handles this with the guard)
⚠ Gotcha: using arr[i] != i+1 as the swap guard instead of arr[i] != arr[correct]

Wrong: while (arr[i] != i+1) { swap; } — for a duplicate like [1, 2, 2, 4], at i=2 arr[2]=2 and correct=1, arr[1]=2. The condition arr[i] != i+1 is true (2 ≠ 3), so you swap arr[2] with arr[1] — swapping two identical 2s — forever.

Correct: if (arr[i] != arr[correct]) — if the destination already holds the same value, swapping changes nothing; advance instead.

One-line rule: guard the swap with arr[i] != arr[arr[i]-1], not arr[i] != i+1.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. Why is the total number of swaps at most n−1, even though there is a while loop nested inside the walk over n elements?
  2. After cyclic_sort completes on an array with one duplicate, what will the sorted array look like at the position of the duplicate?
  3. How would you adapt cyclic sort to handle values in range [0, n−1] instead of [1, n]?
When you see: find missing/duplicate in a 1-to-N array with O(1) space → cyclic sort: swap arr[i] to arr[arr[i]−1] until in place; guard with arr[i] != arr[correct]
  • First missing positive → cyclic sort then find first i where arr[i] != i+1
  • All duplicates → after sort, collect indices where arr[i] != i+1
  • Values in [0..n-1] → change correct = arr[i] (not arr[i]-1)
Pattern 24 In-Place: Dutch National Flag (3-Way Partition)
① Background

Sorting an array with three distinct values (0, 1, 2) in a single pass with O(1) space is the Dutch National Flag problem, named after the three-colour flag by Edsger Dijkstra who posed it in the 1970s. A two-pass approach is easy: count zeros, ones, and twos, then overwrite. But that reads the array twice. The one-pass version uses three pointers that maintain four invariant regions simultaneously.

The regions are: [0..low-1] contains only 0s; [low..mid-1] contains only 1s; [mid..high] is the unknown region; [high+1..n-1] contains only 2s. The mid pointer walks through the unknown region. When it finds a 0, it swaps with the low boundary (both low and mid advance because the element swapped in from low was previously classified). When it finds a 2, it swaps with the high boundary and only decrements high — the element arriving from the right has not been examined yet, so mid does not advance.

The asymmetry between handling 0s and 2s is the most common source of bugs. When swapping with low, the element that was at low had already been processed (it was a 1 or a 0 that got correctly placed); so after the swap, mid can safely advance. When swapping with high, the element that was at high has never been seen; mid must stay and re-examine it.

This pattern generalises beyond three values: any in-place partition into three groups (less-than, equal, greater-than a pivot) uses the same three-pointer structure, which is the basis of the three-way partition step in quicksort for arrays with many duplicate values.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 75 — Sort Colors — textbook Dutch National Flag; values are 0, 1, 2.
  • LeetCode 905 — Sort Array By Parity — two-way partition (even/odd); simplification of DNF with only two regions.
  • Quicksort 3-way partition for arrays with duplicates — when the pivot value appears many times, DNF partitions into <pivot / =pivot / >pivot in O(n).
  • Embedded: segregate sensor readings into below-threshold / normal / above-threshold — real-time systems classify readings into three health zones; DNF does it in a single pass.
  • Packet classification: sort network packets into drop / forward / inspect queues — three-priority classification maps directly to DNF's three-region invariant.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [2, 0, 1]. Goal: sort to [0, 1, 2] in one pass. Three pointers: low=0 (next 0 slot), mid=0 (current element), high=2 (next 2 slot from right).

arr: 2 0 1 low=0, mid=0, high=2 [unknown region: mid..high] Step 1: arr[mid]=arr[0]=2. It belongs at the RIGHT end. Swap arr[mid] with arr[high]: arr[0] ↔ arr[2] → [1, 0, 2] high-- → high=1. DON'T advance mid (arr[0]=1 unexamined). arr: 1 0 2 low=0, mid=0, high=1 Step 2: arr[mid]=arr[0]=1. It belongs in the MIDDLE (keep as-is). mid++ → mid=1. arr: 1 0 2 low=0, mid=1, high=1 Step 3: arr[mid]=arr[1]=0. It belongs at the LEFT end. Swap arr[mid] with arr[low]: arr[1] ↔ arr[0] → [0, 1, 2] low++ → low=1. mid++ → mid=2. mid=2 > high=1 → stop. Result: [0, 1, 2] ✓

Key asymmetry: after swapping with high, mid does NOT advance (the incoming element is unknown). After swapping with low, mid DOES advance (the incoming element from the "1s zone" was already examined).

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Sort array of 0s, 1s, and 2s in a single pass, O(n) time O(1) space. */
void dutch_flag(int *arr, int n) {
    int low  = 0;      /* boundary: arr[0..low-1] are all 0s */
    int mid  = 0;      /* current element under examination */
    int high = n - 1;  /* boundary: arr[high+1..n-1] are all 2s */

    while (mid <= high) {
        if (arr[mid] == 0) {
            /* 0 belongs at the left boundary.
               The element at arr[low] was a 1 (already processed), so after
               the swap we can advance BOTH low and mid safely. */
            int tmp = arr[low]; arr[low] = arr[mid]; arr[mid] = tmp;
            low++;
            mid++;
        } else if (arr[mid] == 2) {
            /* 2 belongs at the right boundary.
               The element arriving from arr[high] is UNKNOWN — we have not
               examined it yet. Advance high but NOT mid; re-examine arr[mid]. */
            int tmp = arr[high]; arr[high] = arr[mid]; arr[mid] = tmp;
            high--;
        } else {
            /* arr[mid] == 1: already in the correct middle region. Just advance. */
            mid++;
        }
    }
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [2, 0, 2, 1, 1, 0], low=0, mid=0, high=5 mid | arr[mid] | action | arr state | low | high ----|----------|---------------------------------|------------------|-----|----- 0 | 2 | swap(0,5), high-- | [0,0,2,1,1,2] | 0 | 4 0 | 0 | swap(0,0)=noop, low++, mid++ | [0,0,2,1,1,2] | 1 | 4 1 | 0 | swap(1,1)=noop, low++, mid++ | [0,0,2,1,1,2] | 2 | 4 2 | 2 | swap(2,4), high-- | [0,0,1,1,2,2] | 2 | 3 2 | 1 | mid++ | [0,0,1,1,2,2] | 2 | 3 3 | 1 | mid++ | [0,0,1,1,2,2] | 2 | 3 4 | mid>high | stop Result: [0,0,1,1,2,2] ✓
⚠ Gotcha: advancing mid after swapping with high

Wrong: adding mid++ after the swap-with-high — this skips the examination of the newly arrived element, which could be a 0 or 2 that still needs to be classified.

Correct: only decrement high; do not touch mid. The next loop iteration will re-examine arr[mid] with the newly arrived element.

One-line rule: swap-with-low → advance both low and mid; swap-with-high → advance only high, not mid.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. When mid encounters a 0 and swaps with low, why is it safe to advance mid immediately? What was at arr[low] before the swap?
  2. What is the invariant of the four regions [0..low-1], [low..mid-1], [mid..high], [high+1..n-1] at the start of each loop iteration?
  3. How would you extend this to a four-way partition (0, 1, 2, 3)?
When you see: sort array with exactly 3 distinct values in one pass → Dutch National Flag: low/mid/high pointers; swap-with-low advances both, swap-with-high advances only high
  • Two-way partition (even/odd, negative/positive) → same idea with only two pointers
  • Quicksort with many duplicates → DNF 3-way partition around pivot avoids O(n²) worst case
  • Generalise to k values → k-way stable partition or multiple passes of DNF
Pattern 25 In-Place: Rotate Array Right by K
① Background

Rotating an array right by K moves the last K elements to the front. [1,2,3,4,5] rotated right by 2 gives [4,5,1,2,3]. The naive approach copies the last K elements to a temporary buffer, shifts the rest right by K, then writes the buffer back: O(n) time but O(k) extra space. For very large arrays under memory constraints, that extra space is unacceptable.

The three-reversal trick achieves O(n) time with O(1) extra space. The insight is algebraic: reversing the whole array then reversing each of the two segments separately is equivalent to a rotation. For right-by-K: reverse all, then reverse [0..K-1], then reverse [K..n-1]. This looks magical but can be verified with any small example in under a minute.

Why three reversals? After reversing the whole array, the last K elements (which should end up at the front) are now at the front but in reverse order. Reversing [0..K-1] puts them in the correct order. The remaining n−K elements (which should end up at the back) are also reversed and in the second segment; reversing [K..n-1] restores their order.

The K mod n normalisation is mandatory: rotating by exactly n positions returns the array to its original state, and rotating by n+2 is identical to rotating by 2. Without normalisation, step 2 would reverse [0..K-1] with K > n, an out-of-bounds operation.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 189 — Rotate Array — textbook application of the three-reversal trick.
  • LeetCode 186 — Reverse Words in a String II — reverse all words, then reverse each individual word — same three-reversal structure.
  • Circular buffer: advance the read pointer by K positions — rotating the view of a circular buffer by K aligns the logical start; the three-reversal trick does this in-place.
  • Cryptography: Caesar cipher key rotation on a key schedule array — periodic rotation of a key array by one position is a common operation in symmetric cipher scheduling.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5], rotate right by k = 2. Goal: [4, 5, 1, 2, 3].

Original: [1, 2, 3, 4, 5] Step 1 — reverse whole array: [1,2,3,4,5] → [5,4,3,2,1] Step 2 — reverse first k=2 elements [0..1]: [5,4,3,2,1] → [4,5,3,2,1] (the last-k=2 elements 4,5 are now at the front in the right order) Step 3 — reverse remaining [k..n-1] = [2..4]: [4,5,3,2,1] → [4,5,1,2,3] ✓

After step 1, the last-k elements (originally [4,5]) are at positions [0..k-1] but reversed ([5,4]). Reversing [0..k-1] in step 2 fixes their order. The original first (n-k) elements are at [k..n-1] but reversed; step 3 fixes them.

Normalisation: what if k = 7 for n = 5? 7 mod 5 = 2. Rotating by 7 is identical to rotating by 2. Always apply k = k % n first; if k becomes 0, the array is already correct.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Reverse arr[lo..hi] in place. */
static void rev(int *arr, int lo, int hi) {
    while (lo < hi) {
        int tmp = arr[lo]; arr[lo] = arr[hi]; arr[hi] = tmp;
        lo++; hi--;
    }
}

/* Rotate arr right by k positions in-place, O(n) time O(1) space. */
void rotate_right(int *arr, int n, int k) {
    /* Normalise: rotating by n is a no-op; rotating by k > n equals rotating by k%n. */
    k = k % n;
    if (k == 0) return;  /* nothing to do */

    rev(arr, 0, n - 1);  /* step 1: reverse the entire array */
    rev(arr, 0, k - 1);  /* step 2: un-reverse the first k (now-correct) elements */
    rev(arr, k, n - 1);  /* step 3: un-reverse the remaining n-k elements */
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [1,2,3,4,5], n=5, k=2 k = 2 % 5 = 2 (no change) rev(arr, 0, 4): [1,2,3,4,5] → [5,4,3,2,1] rev(arr, 0, 1): [5,4,...] → [4,5,3,2,1] rev(arr, 2, 4): [...3,2,1] → [4,5,1,2,3] ✓ k=7 case: k = 7%5 = 2. Same result. k=5 case: k = 5%5 = 0. return immediately (no change). ✓
⚠ Gotcha: missing k = k % n normalisation

Wrong: if k = 7 and n = 5, step 2 calls rev(arr, 0, 6) which is out of bounds (index 6 does not exist), causing undefined behaviour.

Correct: always normalise: k = k % n; before any reversals. If k becomes 0, return early.

One-line rule: always apply k %= n first; rotating by a multiple of n is always a no-op.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. Trace the three-reversal trick for left rotation by k (instead of right). What changes in the reversal order or segment boundaries?
  2. Why must you apply k = k % n before checking if k == 0? What happens if n divides k exactly?
  3. Can you achieve the same rotation using a single cycle-following approach (placing each element directly in its final position)? What are the trade-offs?
When you see: rotate array right by K in-place → k %= n; reverse all; reverse [0..k-1]; reverse [k..n-1]
  • Left rotation by k → reverse [0..k-1]; reverse [k..n-1]; reverse all
  • Reverse words in a string → reverse whole string, then reverse each word in place
  • Rotate matrix rows → apply the same three-reversal logic row by row
Pattern 26 In-Place: Reverse Segments
① Background

Reversing a contiguous segment of an array or string is the atomic building block underlying rotate (P25), reverse-words-in-string, palindrome check, and countless other in-place transformations. The two-pointer swap is so simple that developers often write it inline without thinking, which is also where bugs appear — off-by-one on the segment boundaries, incorrect loop termination, or the XOR-swap self-corruption trap.

The algorithm walks two pointers from both ends of the segment toward the center, swapping at each step. When they meet (or cross), every pair has been swapped exactly once and the segment is reversed. The middle element of an odd-length segment needs no swap — when lo == hi it is already in place and the lo < hi condition stops the loop before touching it.

The XOR swap trick (a ^= b; b ^= a; a ^= b) is a common "clever" alternative to using a temporary variable. It works when a and b are distinct variables or different memory locations. But when a and b are the same memory location (lo == hi, self-swap), all three XOR operations cancel and the value becomes zero. Never use XOR swap without a guard, and prefer the temporary-variable version for clarity.

The reverse-words application demonstrates why segment reversal is powerful: reverse the entire string to bring words to approximately their final positions (but each word is itself reversed), then reverse each individual word to restore its characters. Two passes of the same O(1)-space primitive handle what would otherwise require O(n) extra space to shift words.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 344 — Reverse String — direct application on the whole array.
  • LeetCode 151 — Reverse Words in a String — reverse all, then reverse each word; two uses of the same segment-reverse primitive.
  • LeetCode 189 — Rotate Array — three calls to segment reverse (P25 depends on this as a subroutine).
  • Palindrome check: verify arr[lo..hi] reads the same forwards and backwards — the two-pointer inward walk with comparison instead of swap checks palindrome in O(n) time O(1) space.
  • Firmware: in-place reversal of a sensor reading buffer before transmission — embedded systems often need to reverse byte order (big/little endian swap) without heap allocation.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Use arr = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5], reverse [1..3] (elements 2, 3, 4). Expected result at those positions: [4, 3, 2].

arr: 1 [2 3 4] 5 lo hi lo=1, hi=3 Step 1: lo=1, hi=3. arr[1]=2, arr[3]=4. Swap → arr: 1 [4 3 2] 5. lo=2, hi=2. Step 2: lo=2 == hi=2. Loop condition lo < hi is FALSE. Stop. Result: [1, 4, 3, 2, 5] ✓ (middle element 3 was untouched — already in place)

The loop terminates naturally on odd-length segments when lo == hi: the condition lo < hi fails, so the middle element is never swapped — it is already in its correct position.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Reverse arr[lo..hi] in place, O(hi-lo+1) time, O(1) space. */
void reverse_segment(int *arr, int lo, int hi) {
    while (lo < hi) {
        /* Use a temporary variable — never XOR swap when lo and hi might be equal
           (same memory location would zero out the value). */
        int tmp  = arr[lo];
        arr[lo]  = arr[hi];
        arr[hi]  = tmp;
        lo++;
        hi--;
    }
}

/* Reverse all words in a null-terminated string in-place.
   "the sky is blue" → "blue is sky the"  */
void reverse_words(char *s) {
    int n = 0;
    while (s[n]) n++;  /* find length */

    /* Step 1: reverse entire string → "eulb si yks eht" */
    int lo = 0, hi = n - 1;
    while (lo < hi) {
        char tmp = s[lo]; s[lo] = s[hi]; s[hi] = tmp; lo++; hi--;
    }

    /* Step 2: reverse each word to restore its character order */
    int start = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) {
        if (s[i] == ' ' || s[i] == '\0') {
            lo = start; hi = i - 1;
            while (lo < hi) {
                char tmp = s[lo]; s[lo] = s[hi]; s[hi] = tmp; lo++; hi--;
            }
            start = i + 1;
        }
    }
}
⑤ Dry Run
reverse_segment([1,2,3,4,5], lo=1, hi=3): lo | hi | arr[lo] | arr[hi] | swap result | next lo | next hi ---|----|---------|---------|--------------------|---------|-------- 1 | 3 | 2 | 4 | [1,4,3,2,5] | 2 | 2 lo=2 not < hi=2 → stop result: [1, 4, 3, 2, 5] ✓ reverse_words("the sky is blue"): n=14 Step 1: reverse all → "eulb si yks eht" Step 2: reverse each word: "eulb" → "blue", "si" → "is", "yks" → "sky", "eht" → "the" result: "blue is sky the" ✓
⚠ Gotcha: using XOR swap without checking lo != hi

Wrong: arr[lo] ^= arr[hi]; arr[hi] ^= arr[lo]; arr[lo] ^= arr[hi]; — if lo == hi (same address), all three XORs cancel: a ^= a = 0, 0 ^= 0 = 0, 0 ^= 0 = 0. The middle element becomes 0.

Correct: use a temporary variable: int tmp = arr[lo]; arr[lo] = arr[hi]; arr[hi] = tmp; — always safe regardless of whether lo == hi.

One-line rule: never use XOR swap in a reverse loop; use a temp variable and let lo < hi guard the middle element.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. For an odd-length segment [lo..hi], how many swap operations does the reverse perform? For even-length?
  2. Explain why reversing the whole string and then reversing each word restores the correct word order. Why doesn't the double reversal restore the original string?
  3. If you wanted to check whether a segment is a palindrome (instead of reversing it), how would you modify the two-pointer walk?
When you see: reverse a subarray or string segment in-place → two pointers from both ends, swap with temp variable, stop when lo ≥ hi
  • Reverse words in string → reverse all, then reverse each word
  • Rotate array → three calls to reverse_segment (P25)
  • Palindrome check → same two-pointer walk but compare instead of swap
Pattern 27 Sorting: Custom Comparator with qsort
① Background

C's standard library qsort() is a general-purpose sort that works on any array of any element type by accepting a caller-supplied comparator function. The comparator receives two const void * pointers — each pointing to one element in the array — and must return a negative integer if the first element should precede the second, zero if they are equivalent, or a positive integer if the second should precede the first.

The most important skill is correct pointer casting. The comparator's parameters are void pointers to array elements, not to values. For an array of ints, each void pointer points to an int; cast to const int * and dereference. For an array of char pointers (string array), each void pointer points to a char *; cast to const char ** and dereference to get the string. Getting this double indirection wrong is the single most common qsort bug.

The subtraction shortcut for integers (return *a - *b) is tempting but subtly wrong: if a = INT_MAX and b = -1, the subtraction INT_MAX - (-1) = INT_MAX + 1 overflows to a negative number, silently reversing the sort order for that pair. Always use explicit comparisons with -1/0/1 returns for integer comparators.

qsort is not stable — equal elements may appear in any relative order after sorting. If stability is required (preserving original order for equal elements), implement a stable sort or use the index-comparison trick: compare by primary key first, break ties by original index.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 179 — Largest Number — sort integers by which concatenation order produces a larger number; custom comparator compares s1+s2 vs s2+s1.
  • LeetCode 56 — Merge Intervals — sort intervals by start time before merging; custom comparator on struct fields.
  • C standard library bsearch() — same comparator contract as qsort; the comparator you write for qsort works unchanged in bsearch.
  • Embedded: sort sensor records by timestamp then by sensor ID — multi-key sort using a struct comparator with primary/secondary key checks.
  • Kernel: sort process list by priority then by PID for deterministic scheduling output — OS diagnostic tools sort process tables; custom comparator handles the multi-field ordering.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

qsort signature: void qsort(void *base, size_t n, size_t size, int (*cmp)(const void *, const void *)). qsort calls cmp(p, q) where p and q are pointers into the array, each offset by size bytes from the base.

Array of ints: int arr[] = {5, 2, 8} Memory layout: [5][2][8] (each element is sizeof(int)=4 bytes) qsort calls cmp(&arr[0], &arr[1]): a = (void*)&arr[0] → cast to (const int*) → dereference → 5 b = (void*)&arr[1] → cast to (const int*) → dereference → 2 5 > 2 → return 1 → qsort swaps them → [2][5][8] Array of strings: char *words[] = {"cat", "ant", "bee"} Memory layout: [ptr→"cat"][ptr→"ant"][ptr→"bee"] qsort calls cmp(&words[0], &words[1]): a = (void*)&words[0] → cast to (const char**) → dereference → ptr→"cat" b = (void*)&words[1] → cast to (const char**) → dereference → ptr→"ant" compare "cat" vs "ant" → 'c' > 'a' → return 1

For a string array, the cast is TWO levels of indirection: const char **. The void pointer points to a char* (the string pointer), not to the character data directly. A single cast to const char* would give you a pointer to the pointer, not a pointer to the string.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>

/* Sort integers ascending — safe version with explicit comparison. */
int cmp_int_asc(const void *a, const void *b) {
    int x = *(const int *)a;
    int y = *(const int *)b;
    /* Do NOT write: return x - y;  (overflows when x=INT_MAX, y=-1) */
    if (x < y) return -1;
    if (x > y) return  1;
    return 0;
}

/* Sort an array of C strings lexicographically. */
int cmp_str(const void *a, const void *b) {
    /* a and b are pointers to elements of a char* array, so they are char**. */
    const char *sa = *(const char **)a;
    const char *sb = *(const char **)b;
    return strcmp(sa, sb);  /* strcmp already returns negative/0/positive */
}

/* Sort struct by primary key (x), then secondary key (y). */
typedef struct { int x, y; } Point;
int cmp_point(const void *a, const void *b) {
    const Point *pa = (const Point *)a;
    const Point *pb = (const Point *)b;
    if (pa->x != pb->x) {
        /* Primary key differs: safe subtraction (x values assumed in range) */
        return (pa->x > pb->x) - (pa->x < pb->x);  /* branchless -1/0/1 */
    }
    return (pa->y > pb->y) - (pa->y < pb->y);
}

/* Usage: */
void example(void) {
    int arr[] = {5, 2, 8, 1, 9};
    qsort(arr, 5, sizeof(int), cmp_int_asc);
    /* arr is now [1, 2, 5, 8, 9] */
}
⑤ Dry Run
cmp_int_asc called by qsort with a=&5, b=&2: x=5, y=2 → x > y → return 1 → qsort places 2 before 5 cmp_str called with a=&"banana", b=&"apple": sa = "banana", sb = "apple" strcmp("banana","apple") → 'b'-'a' = 1 > 0 → return positive → "apple" before "banana" cmp_point on (3,5) vs (3,2): pa->x == pb->x (both 3) → compare y: 5 > 2 → return 1 → (3,2) before (3,5)
⚠ Gotcha: returning x - y for integer comparator

Wrong: return *(int*)a - *(int*)b; — if a=INT_MAX (2147483647) and b=-1, the subtraction overflows to a negative number, making qsort think INT_MAX < -1.

Correct: explicit branching: if (x < y) return -1; if (x > y) return 1; return 0; — or the branchless idiom: return (x > y) - (x < y);

One-line rule: never use subtraction as a comparator return value for integers; always use explicit </> comparisons.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. For a char *words[] array, why is the correct cast (const char **) and not (const char *)?
  2. qsort is not stable. If you sort an array of structs by one field and need to preserve original order for ties, what comparator technique achieves stability?
  3. Can you use the same comparator function for both qsort and bsearch on the same array? What would bsearch pass as its key parameter?
When you see: sort by custom rule in C → qsort + comparator: cast void* correctly, return −1/0/1 with explicit comparisons (never subtraction)
  • Integer array → cast to (const int *), compare with if/else or (x>y)-(x<y)
  • String (char *) array → cast to (const char **), dereference, use strcmp()
  • Struct array → cast to (const MyStruct *), compare fields directly
Pattern 28 Sorting: Merge Overlapping Intervals
① Background

Given a collection of intervals that may overlap, find the minimal set of non-overlapping intervals that covers the same range. The brute-force approach compares every pair of intervals to find overlaps: O(n²). The sorting-based approach reduces this to O(n log n) sort followed by a single O(n) scan.

The key insight is that after sorting by start time, two intervals can only overlap if they are adjacent in the sorted order. If [a, b] is fully before [c, d] (b < c) with all intervals sorted by start, then nothing between them in the sorted list can overlap with [a, b] — because every interval between them starts after a and therefore also after b (if b < c ≤ next_start). So once you have sorted order, a single left-to-right scan with one pointer is sufficient.

The merge step itself has one subtle trap: when two intervals overlap, you extend the current interval's end to max(current.end, next.end) — not just next.end. A contained interval like [1,10] followed by [2,3] must not shrink the end from 10 to 3. Always take the max when extending.

In systems contexts this pattern appears in memory allocator coalescing (merging adjacent free blocks), in calendar applications (computing busy/free time), in log file analysis (finding consecutive error windows), and in network routing table compression (merging overlapping IP prefix ranges).

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 56 — Merge Intervals — textbook application.
  • LeetCode 57 — Insert Interval — insert a new interval into a sorted list, then merge any resulting overlaps.
  • Memory allocator: coalesce adjacent free blocks into one larger block — free block ranges sorted by address; merge overlapping/touching ranges to reduce fragmentation.
  • Network: compress overlapping IP prefix ranges into minimal set — routing table optimisation; overlapping address ranges are merged to reduce table size.
  • Calendar: compute free slots from a list of busy intervals — merge all busy intervals, then free slots are the gaps between merged intervals.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Intervals: [[1,3],[2,6],[8,10],[15,18]]. Sort by start (already sorted here). Walk left to right, maintaining the last merged interval.

result = [[1,3]] (seed with first interval) i=1: [2,6]. Does 2 ≤ 3 (next.start ≤ cur.end)? YES → overlap. Extend: end = max(3, 6) = 6. result = [[1,6]] i=2: [8,10]. Does 8 ≤ 6? NO → no overlap. Start new entry. result = [[1,6], [8,10]] i=3: [15,18]. Does 15 ≤ 10? NO → no overlap. Start new entry. result = [[1,6], [8,10], [15,18]] ✓

The "contained interval" trap: if input were [[1,10],[2,3]], at i=1: 2 ≤ 10, overlap. end = max(10,3) = 10. Without the max, we'd incorrectly shrink [1,10] to [1,3].

④ Full Code Walkthrough
#include <stdlib.h>

typedef struct { int start, end; } Interval;

int cmp_interval(const void *a, const void *b) {
    /* Safe: start values are non-negative so subtraction won't overflow. */
    return ((const Interval *)a)->start - ((const Interval *)b)->start;
}

/* Merge overlapping intervals.  result[] must have room for n entries.
   Returns the count of merged (non-overlapping) intervals. */
int merge_intervals(Interval *ivs, int n, Interval *result) {
    if (n == 0) return 0;

    qsort(ivs, n, sizeof(Interval), cmp_interval);

    int k = 0;
    result[0] = ivs[0];  /* seed: first interval always starts the first merged group */

    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        if (ivs[i].start <= result[k].end) {
            /* Overlap: extend the end to whichever is larger.
               Must use max — the new interval might be contained within the current one. */
            if (ivs[i].end > result[k].end)
                result[k].end = ivs[i].end;
        } else {
            /* No overlap: start a fresh merged interval. */
            k++;
            result[k] = ivs[i];
        }
    }
    return k + 1;  /* k is zero-based index of the last entry */
}
⑤ Dry Run
ivs = [[1,3],[2,6],[8,10],[15,18]] After qsort (already sorted by start): [[1,3],[2,6],[8,10],[15,18]] result[0] = [1,3], k=0 i=1: [2,6]. 2 ≤ 3 → overlap. max(3,6)=6 → result[0]=[1,6] i=2: [8,10]. 8 > 6 → no overlap. k=1, result[1]=[8,10] i=3: [15,18]. 15 > 10 → no overlap. k=2, result[2]=[15,18] return 3 → [[1,6],[8,10],[15,18]] ✓ Contained interval trap: ivs = [[1,10],[2,3]] result[0]=[1,10], k=0 i=1: [2,3]. 2 ≤ 10 → overlap. max(10,3)=10 → result[0]=[1,10] ✓ (not shrunk)
⚠ Gotcha: writing result[k].end = ivs[i].end instead of max

Wrong: result[k].end = ivs[i].end; — if the next interval is contained within the current one ([2,3] inside [1,10]), this shrinks the end from 10 to 3.

Correct: if (ivs[i].end > result[k].end) result[k].end = ivs[i].end; — only extend, never shrink.

One-line rule: when merging intervals, always take max(current.end, next.end).

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. Why is it sufficient to only compare each new interval against the LAST merged interval (result[k]) rather than all previous merged intervals?
  2. What is the overlap condition — is it next.start <= cur.end or next.start < cur.end? When does the choice matter?
  3. How would you solve "insert interval" (LeetCode 57): insert a new interval into an already-sorted non-overlapping list and merge any resulting overlaps?
When you see: merge overlapping intervals → sort by start; walk left-to-right; extend end = max(cur.end, next.end) if overlap (next.start ≤ cur.end), else start new
  • Find free time slots → merge all busy intervals, gaps between merged = free
  • Insert interval → binary-search insert position, then merge with neighbours
  • Count overlapping pairs → sort by start, sweep with max-end tracking
Pattern 29 Sorting: Meeting Rooms / Interval Overlap Check
① Background

Two related problems: (1) can one person attend all meetings (no overlaps)? and (2) what is the minimum number of rooms needed for all meetings to happen simultaneously? Both have O(n log n) solutions built on the same sort-then-scan skeleton.

For the "can attend all" question, sort by start time. After sorting, two intervals can only conflict if they are adjacent in sorted order. A single linear scan checking adjacent pairs is sufficient.

For minimum rooms, the chronological sweep approach: sort start times and end times independently into two arrays. Walk through start times in order. For each new meeting starting, check if any previous meeting has already ended (by comparing with the current minimum end time). If yes, reuse that room (advance the end pointer); if no, allocate a new room. The peak room count at any point is the answer.

This sweep-line technique applies to any resource-counting problem over overlapping intervals — OS thread scheduling, CPU core allocation, network connection tracking, and elevator dispatch scheduling.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 252 — Meeting Rooms (can attend all?) — sort by start, check adjacent pairs for overlap; first overlap found → return false.
  • LeetCode 253 — Meeting Rooms II (min rooms) — two sorted arrays + two-pointer sweep for peak concurrent count.
  • OS scheduling: max concurrent threads at any instant — thread execution intervals → sweep gives peak thread count for capacity planning.
  • Network: maximum simultaneous TCP connections in a time window — connection start/end times; sweep to find peak concurrency.
  • LeetCode 435 — Non-overlapping Intervals (greedy removal) — sort by end time; greedy skip of overlapping intervals gives minimum removals.
③ Building the Logic From Scratch

Meetings: [[0,30],[5,10],[15,20]]. Sort starts: [0,5,15]. Sort ends: [10,20,30].

s=0: starts[0]=0. Compare with ends[e=0]=10. 0 < 10 → new room. rooms=1. s=1: starts[1]=5. Compare with ends[e=0]=10. 5 < 10 → new room. rooms=2. ← peak s=2: starts[2]=15. Compare with ends[e=0]=10. 15 ≥ 10 → meeting ended, reuse (e=1). rooms=2.

We never need to know WHICH meeting ended — only whether any meeting ended before the new one starts. The sorted end-times array with a single pointer e always gives us the earliest ending meeting in O(1).

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Can one person attend all meetings? Returns 1 if yes, 0 if any overlap. */
int can_attend_all(Interval *ivs, int n) {
    qsort(ivs, n, sizeof(Interval), cmp_interval);  /* sort by start time */

    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        /* After sorting, only adjacent intervals can overlap.
           Use < not <= if end-time is exclusive (half-open interval convention). */
        if (ivs[i].start < ivs[i-1].end)
            return 0;  /* this meeting starts before the previous one ends */
    }
    return 1;
}

static int cmp_int(const void *a, const void *b) {
    int x = *(const int *)a, y = *(const int *)b;
    return (x > y) - (x < y);
}

/* Minimum conference rooms needed for all meetings. */
int min_rooms(Interval *ivs, int n) {
    int *starts = malloc(n * sizeof(int));
    int *ends   = malloc(n * sizeof(int));
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { starts[i] = ivs[i].start; ends[i] = ivs[i].end; }
    qsort(starts, n, sizeof(int), cmp_int);
    qsort(ends,   n, sizeof(int), cmp_int);

    int rooms = 0, max_rooms = 0, e = 0;
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (starts[s] < ends[e]) {
            rooms++;  /* new meeting starts before earliest end: need another room */
        } else {
            e++;      /* an earlier meeting ended: reuse its room */
        }
        if (rooms > max_rooms) max_rooms = rooms;
    }
    free(starts); free(ends);
    return max_rooms;
}
⑤ Dry Run
ivs = [[0,30],[5,10],[15,20]], n=3 can_attend_all (sorted by start: [0,30],[5,10],[15,20]): i=1: ivs[1].start=5 < ivs[0].end=30 → return 0 ✓ (overlap at t=5) min_rooms: starts=[0,5,15] ends=[10,20,30] e=0, rooms=0 s=0: starts[0]=0 vs ends[0]=10: 0<10 → rooms=1, max=1 s=1: starts[1]=5 vs ends[0]=10: 5<10 → rooms=2, max=2 s=2: starts[2]=15 vs ends[0]=10: 15≥10 → e=1, rooms=2 (check again) starts[2]=15 vs ends[1]=20: only one check per s rooms stays 2, max=2 return 2 ✓
⚠ Gotcha: using < vs ≤ in the overlap check

Half-open intervals [start, end): meetings [0,10] and [10,20] don't overlap (one ends exactly when the other starts). Use start < prev_end.

Closed intervals [start, end]: meetings [0,10] and [10,20] do overlap at time 10. Use start <= prev_end.

One-line rule: read the problem's interval convention carefully; the single-character difference < vs <= determines whether touching endpoints count as overlap.

⑥ Confidence Check
Can you answer these without looking at the code?
  1. In min_rooms, why is it sufficient to sort start times and end times into separate arrays rather than keeping them paired? What information do we not need?
  2. After the sweep loop, why does rooms (not max_rooms) sometimes stay elevated? Is that a bug?
  3. How would you solve "minimum number of people needed to attend all meetings" if each person can attend multiple non-overlapping meetings? Is it the same as min_rooms?
When you see: interval overlap detection or peak concurrent count → sort by start (for overlap check) or sort starts+ends separately (for min rooms sweep)
  • Can attend all → sort by start, check adjacent: ivs[i].start < ivs[i-1].end
  • Min rooms → sort starts and ends separately; two-pointer sweep tracks rooms in use
  • Non-overlapping intervals (min removals) → sort by end time; greedy keep
Pattern 30 Greedy: Jump Game (Can You Reach the End?)
① Background

You have an array of non-negative integers. Each value tells you the maximum number of steps you may take forward from that index. Starting at index 0, can you reach the last index? A naive approach tries every possible path recursively — exponential time, completely impractical for large arrays. A DP table that stores "reachable[i] = true/false" works in O(n²) but wastes effort.

The greedy insight is that you only need one number: max_reach — the farthest index you can touch given every position visited so far. You never need to remember which positions contributed to it; you just need the frontier. If you walk left to right and your current index ever exceeds the frontier, you're stuck. That's it.

The reason greedy works here is a monotonicity property: if you can reach index j, you can definitely reach all indices between 0 and j (you just choose smaller jumps). So reachability is a contiguous prefix of the array. Tracking the right edge of that prefix (max_reach) is sufficient to answer the question.

In practice this appears whenever a system has "capacity" or "range" at each node — battery-powered rovers, memory page-table traversal with variable-length entries, signal relay chains where each station has a broadcast radius, or parser state machines where each token can advance the parse cursor by a variable amount.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 55 — Jump Gamecanonical reachability form; arr[i] = max steps
  • LeetCode 1306 — Jump Game IIIsame idea extended to bidirectional jumps with visited tracking
  • Network relay chain reachabilityeach router has a max-hop radius; can a packet reach node N?
  • Rover mission planningeach waypoint has remaining battery; can rover reach the base station?
  • Variable-length instruction decodeeach opcode says how many bytes to advance; can the PC reach a target address?
  • File chunked-transfer reassemblyeach received chunk extends the contiguous coverage frontier; is the file fully covered?
  • Game level reachabilityeach platform specifies max jump height; can the player reach the exit platform?
③ Building the Logic from Scratch

Start with the question: "Can I ever get to index n-1?" We stand at index 0. From 0 we can reach any index up to arr[0]. From each of those we can push the frontier further. We need to know: does the frontier ever reach or pass n-1 before we walk off it?

The frontier model: Imagine painting all reachable indices green. Initially only index 0 is green (we start there). At each green index i, we can paint everything from i+1 to i+arr[i] green as well. The frontier is the rightmost green index. If we encounter a non-green index while walking left to right, we've hit a gap — unreachable.

arr = [2, 3, 1, 0, 4]
idx:   0  1  2  3  4

Walk left to right, tracking max_reach:
i=0: reach=0+2=2,  max_reach=2  [0,1,2] are green
i=1: reach=1+3=4,  max_reach=4  [0..4] are green — but wait, let's continue
i=2: reach=2+1=3,  max_reach=4  no change
i=3: reach=3+0=3,  max_reach=4  no change
i=4: 4 <= max_reach=4, reach=4+4=8, max_reach=8 >= n-1=4 → YES

arr = [3, 2, 1, 0, 4]
i=0: reach=3, max_reach=3  [0..3] green
i=1: reach=3, max_reach=3
i=2: reach=3, max_reach=3
i=3: reach=3, max_reach=3  (arr[3]=0, no extension)
i=4: 4 > max_reach=3 → STUCK → NO

Aha moment: you don't need to track which specific path reaches each index. The single scalar max_reach captures the entire reachable prefix. Walk the array once; update the frontier; check if you fell off it.

Pseudocode:

max_reach = 0
for i from 0 to n-1:
    if i > max_reach: return NO   // gap found — stuck
    max_reach = max(max_reach, i + arr[i])
    if max_reach >= n-1: return YES
return YES
④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Returns 1 if last index is reachable, 0 if not. */
int can_jump(int *arr, int n) {
    int max_reach = 0;       /* right edge of the reachable prefix */

    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (i > max_reach)   /* walked past the frontier — this index is unreachable */
            return 0;

        int new_reach = i + arr[i];
        if (new_reach > max_reach)
            max_reach = new_reach;   /* extend the frontier from this position */

        if (max_reach >= n - 1)     /* frontier already covers the last index */
            return 1;
    }
    return 1;   /* survived the entire walk without hitting a gap */
}
⑤ Dry Run
Normal case: arr = [2, 3, 1, 1, 4], n = 5 i | arr[i] | new_reach | max_reach | note ----+--------+-----------+-----------+----- 0 | 2 | 0+2=2 | 2 | start 1 | 3 | 1+3=4 | 4 | frontier jumps to 4 2 | 1 | 2+1=3 | 4 | no change 3 | 1 | 3+1=4 | 4 | no change 4 | 4 | 4+4=8 | 8 | max_reach>=4 → return 1 Stuck case: arr = [3, 2, 1, 0, 4], n = 5 i | arr[i] | new_reach | max_reach | note ----+--------+-----------+-----------+----- 0 | 3 | 0+3=3 | 3 | can reach 0..3 1 | 2 | 1+2=3 | 3 | no change 2 | 1 | 2+1=3 | 3 | no change 3 | 0 | 3+0=3 | 3 | zero jump — frontier stalls 4 | — | — | — | 4 > max_reach=3 → return 0 Edge: arr = [0], n = 1 Loop: i=0, 0 <= max_reach=0, max_reach stays 0, 0 >= n-1=0 → return 1 Single element — already at the end, always reachable.
⑥ Gotcha
⚠ Wrong: checking arr[i] == 0 to detect a stuck position
/* WRONG */
for (int i = 0; i < n - 1; i++) {
    if (arr[i] == 0) return 0;   /* assumes any zero means stuck */
}

What breaks: arr = [2, 0, 1] — index 1 has arr[1]=0 but you can jump over it from index 0. This returns 0 incorrectly; the answer is 1.

/* CORRECT */
if (i > max_reach) return 0;   /* stuck only if this index itself is unreachable */

Why it works: arr[i]=0 just means you can't extend the frontier from position i. You might have already extended it past i from an earlier position. Only when the frontier falls behind your current index is a gap confirmed.

Rule: A zero at position i is a problem only if no earlier position could jump past i.

⑦ Confidence Check

1. If max_reach already exceeds n-1 before the loop finishes, why is it safe to return early rather than continuing the scan?

2. Why can't you replace the i > max_reach check with arr[i] == 0?

3. What value should max_reach start at, and what does it represent conceptually — an index, a count, or something else?

⑧ Trigger
When you see "can you reach the end with variable jump lengths" → track a single max_reach frontier; return false the moment your walk index exceeds it.
Also fires for: "is node N reachable given per-node range values" · "does coverage ever reach position P" · "can a relay chain bridge a gap"
Pattern 31 Greedy: Jump Game II (Minimum Jumps to Reach End)
① Background

Same array as Jump Game I — each element is the maximum jump you can make from that index — but now the question changes from "can you reach the end?" to "what is the minimum number of jumps to reach the end?" Assuming reachability is guaranteed (problem constraint), you need an efficient count.

A brute-force approach tries all possible jump lengths at each position and recurses, yielding exponential time. A DP approach fills a table of minimum jumps for each index in O(n²). But greedy does it in O(n) and constant space, with a key insight borrowed from BFS: think of each "jump count level" as a layer. All positions reachable in exactly k jumps form layer k. Walk through the current layer, find the farthest you can push into layer k+1, and count layers to the end.

The BFS analogy is exact: the starting index is the source; the last index is the sink; edges connect index i to all indices in [i+1, i+arr[i]]. Minimum jumps = shortest path in this implicit graph. BFS on this graph is O(n) because every index is visited at most once across all layers.

This appears in firmware loaders that chain-load variable-length segments, in link-following where each page has a "max forward links" budget, and in any scheduling problem where you advance through stages with variable-length strides and want the minimum number of phase transitions.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 45 — Jump Game IIcanonical minimum-jumps form
  • LeetCode 1345 — Jump Game IVsame BFS-layer idea extended with hash-linked equal-value jumps
  • Bootloader chain-loadingeach stage specifies max bytes it can hand off; minimum handoff count to reach the kernel
  • Web crawler hop countingeach page has N outbound links; minimum hops to reach target URL
  • Game level minimum moveseach tile allows up to k forward steps; minimum turns to exit
  • Memory garbage collector sweepeach live object has a forward-pointer reach; minimum GC passes to mark all objects
③ Building the Logic from Scratch

BFS layer model: After 0 jumps you're at layer {0}. After 1 jump you can reach layer {1..arr[0]}. After 2 jumps, from each position in layer 1, you extend further. The farthest reachable position after k jumps is the right edge of layer k.

arr = [2, 3, 1, 1, 4]
idx:   0  1  2  3  4

Layer 0 (0 jumps):  [0]              current_end=0
From idx 0: farthest = 0+2 = 2

Hit i==current_end (0): jumps→1, current_end=2

Layer 1 (1 jump):  [1, 2]
From idx 1: farthest = max(2, 1+3) = 4
From idx 2: farthest = max(4, 2+1) = 4

Hit i==current_end (2): jumps→2, current_end=4

Layer 2 (2 jumps): [3, 4]
Loop ends at i=3 (n-1=4 not processed by i<n-1)

→ 2 jumps

Three variables: jumps counts layers; current_end marks the right edge of the current layer; farthest tracks the best right edge for the next layer as we walk.

Aha moment: you don't pick which position in the current layer to jump from — you greedily take the maximum reach achievable from any position in the layer. When you exhaust the current layer (i reaches current_end), the best you could have done is already recorded in farthest.

Pseudocode:

jumps = 0, current_end = 0, farthest = 0
for i from 0 to n-2:            // don't jump FROM the last index
    farthest = max(farthest, i + arr[i])
    if i == current_end:        // current layer exhausted
        jumps++
        current_end = farthest  // advance to next layer
return jumps
④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Minimum jumps to reach the last index. Assumes it is reachable. */
int jump_min(int *arr, int n) {
    int jumps       = 0;   /* layers (BFS levels) traversed so far */
    int current_end = 0;   /* right edge of the current BFS layer */
    int farthest    = 0;   /* best right edge seen while scanning current layer */

    for (int i = 0; i < n - 1; i++) {   /* n-1: no need to jump from the last index */
        int reach = i + arr[i];
        if (reach > farthest) farthest = reach;   /* extend next-layer frontier */

        if (i == current_end) {      /* scanned all of the current layer */
            jumps++;                 /* commit one more jump */
            current_end = farthest; /* the next layer spans up to farthest */
        }
    }
    return jumps;
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr = [2, 3, 1, 1, 4], n = 5 i | arr[i] | farthest | current_end | jumps | note ----+--------+----------+-------------+-------+----- 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 | 0 | start 0 | — | 2 | 2 | 1 | i==cur_end → jump, advance layer 1 | 3 | 4 | 2 | 1 | 1+3=4 2 | 1 | 4 | 2 | 1 | 2+1=3, no change 2 | — | 4 | 4 | 2 | i==cur_end → jump, advance layer 3 | 1 | 4 | 4 | 2 | loop ends at i<n-1=4 return 2 Edge: arr = [0], n = 1 Loop runs 0 times (n-1=0, i starts at 0, condition 0<0 is false). return 0 — already at the end, no jumps needed.
⑥ Gotcha
⚠ Wrong: looping to i < n instead of i < n - 1
/* WRONG */
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    ...
    if (i == current_end) { jumps++; current_end = farthest; }
}

What breaks: When i reaches n-1 (the last index) and current_end happens to equal n-1, the condition fires and jumps is incremented one extra time. You're already at the destination — no jump needed from there.

/* CORRECT */
for (int i = 0; i < n - 1; i++) { ... }

Why it works: You only ever need to count jumps to the last index, never from it. Stopping at n-2 as the last processed index avoids the spurious +1.

Rule: Loop bound is n - 1, not n.

⑦ Confidence Check

1. Why does this algorithm work even though it never explicitly chooses which position in the current layer to jump from?

2. What would go wrong if you updated current_end = farthest immediately whenever farthest grew, instead of only when i == current_end?

3. This is described as "BFS in disguise." What corresponds to the BFS queue, and why don't you need an explicit queue data structure here?

⑧ Trigger
When you see "minimum jumps / minimum steps with variable stride" → maintain a current-layer window; scan it for the farthest reach; increment jump count when the window is exhausted.
Also fires for: "minimum phase transitions across stages with variable-length strides" · "fewest BFS levels to reach a target in a reachability graph"
Pattern 32 Greedy: Gas Station Circular Tour
① Background

N gas stations are arranged in a circle. Station i has gas[i] units of fuel and costs cost[i] to travel to the next station. You start with an empty tank and can begin at any station. Find a starting station from which you can complete the full circuit without running dry, or report that no such station exists.

The brute-force approach tries all N starting points, simulating each circuit in O(n) time — O(n²) total. The greedy observation cuts this to O(n): if you start at station i and fail at station k (tank goes negative), every station between i and k is also an invalid start. Why? If you could have started at some j in [i+1, k] and succeeded, then starting from i you would have had even more gas when you reached j (the surplus from i to j is non-negative since you didn't fail there). Contradiction. So you can skip all of [i, k] and try k+1.

A separate global feasibility check: if total gas < total cost, no solution exists regardless of starting point. This is checked in the same pass via a running total. If the global sum is ≥ 0, exactly one valid starting point exists, and the greedy candidate (the one that survived without going negative) is it.

Real-world parallels: circular pipeline routing where each pumping station has net flow capacity; round-robin task scheduling where each time slot has a net CPU budget; data replication rings where each node can relay a fixed amount of bandwidth per cycle.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 134 — Gas Stationcanonical form; return start index or -1
  • LeetCode 860 — Lemonade Changegreedy feasibility across a sequence with running balance
  • Circular pipeline routingeach pump station has net flow; find the entry point for a complete circuit
  • Round-robin CPU schedulingeach time slot has net slack; find the slot from which the round can complete without starvation
  • Token-bucket relay chainseach relay node refills/drains a shared token pool; find a starting node for a full traversal
  • Network packet round-trip routingeach hop has a forwarding budget; find the starting hop that guarantees round-trip completion
③ Building the Logic from Scratch

Why skipping works: Suppose you start at i and fail at k. For any j in (i, k], the tank when you arrive at j (starting from i) is non-negative — you didn't fail before k. If starting at j was viable, starting at i and skipping to j gives you an even higher tank at j, so you'd also succeed. That means j alone can't fix the failure at k. So j is also invalid.

gas  = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
cost = [3, 4, 5, 1, 2]
net  = [-2,-2,-2, 3, 3]

Starting at 0:
i=0: tank=-2 → fail → start=1, tank=0
i=1: tank=-2 → fail → start=2, tank=0
i=2: tank=-2 → fail → start=3, tank=0
i=3: tank=+3 → ok
i=4: tank=+6 → ok

total_surplus = (-2-2-2+3+3) = 0 >= 0 → return 3 ✓

Verify: start at 3, net gains: +3,+3,-2,-2,-2 = 0
Running: 3, 6, 4, 2, 0 — never negative ✓

Aha moment: the global total check and the candidate identification happen in a single pass. You don't need a second pass to verify the candidate — the math guarantees it.

Pseudocode:

total = 0, tank = 0, start = 0
for i in 0..n-1:
    net = gas[i] - cost[i]
    total += net
    tank  += net
    if tank < 0:
        start = i + 1
        tank  = 0
return total >= 0 ? start : -1
④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Returns start index for a complete circuit, or -1 if impossible. */
int can_complete_circuit(int *gas, int *cost, int n) {
    int total = 0;   /* global net — determines feasibility */
    int tank  = 0;   /* running tank from current start candidate */
    int start = 0;   /* current candidate starting station */

    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        int net = gas[i] - cost[i];
        total += net;
        tank  += net;

        if (tank < 0) {
            /* failed at i: skip all stations up to and including i */
            start = i + 1;   /* fresh candidate: try starting just after failure point */
            tank  = 0;       /* reset tank — new candidate starts empty */
        }
    }

    return total < 0 ? -1 : start;   /* global deficit means no solution; otherwise start is it */
}
⑤ Dry Run
Normal: gas=[1,2,3,4,5], cost=[3,4,5,1,2] i | net | total | tank | start | action ----+-----+-------+------+-------+------- 0 | -2 | -2 | -2 | 0 | tank<0 → start=1, tank=0 1 | -2 | -4 | -2 | 1 | tank<0 → start=2, tank=0 2 | -2 | -6 | -2 | 2 | tank<0 → start=3, tank=0 3 | 3 | -3 | 3 | 3 | ok 4 | 3 | 0 | 6 | 3 | ok total=0 >= 0 → return start=3 ✓ Impossible: gas=[2,3,4], cost=[3,4,5] total = (2-3)+(3-4)+(4-5) = -3 < 0 → return -1 ✓
⑥ Gotcha
⚠ Wrong: forgetting to reset tank when updating start
/* WRONG */
if (tank < 0) {
    start = i + 1;
    /* forgot: tank = 0; */
}

What breaks: The negative balance from the old candidate carries forward into the new candidate's evaluation, making it look worse than it actually is. A station that could complete the circuit from a fresh start will appear to fail.

/* CORRECT */
if (tank < 0) {
    start = i + 1;
    tank  = 0;   /* new candidate starts with an empty tank */
}

Rule: Every time you move the start pointer, reset tank to zero — the new candidate hasn't collected any gas yet.

⑦ Confidence Check

1. Why is it safe to skip all stations between the old start and the failure point, rather than trying each one individually?

2. The algorithm returns the final value of start when total >= 0. Why is this guaranteed to be the correct starting station without a second verification pass?

3. What happens if total == 0 exactly — does that mean one valid start exists, multiple, or none?

⑧ Trigger
When you see "find a starting point for a circular route with net gains/losses" → track running tank; move start past any failure point; return start if global total ≥ 0.
Also fires for: "circular buffer with per-slot credits and debits" · "ring topology routing feasibility"
Pattern 33 Greedy: Assign Cookies (Two-Sorted-Array Matching)
① Background

You have a set of children, each with a minimum greed factor — the smallest cookie size that will satisfy them. You also have a bag of cookies of various sizes. You want to maximize the number of satisfied children. Each child gets at most one cookie; each cookie is used at most once.

The greedy insight is an exchange argument: if you're going to satisfy the least greedy child (smallest g[i]), use the smallest cookie that can satisfy them. Using a bigger cookie wastes it — the bigger cookie might have been able to satisfy someone greedier, but the smaller cookie cannot. So always assign the cheapest sufficient resource to the cheapest sufficient demand.

This exchange argument generalizes to a whole family of two-sorted-array matching problems: match the k smallest "demands" to the k smallest "supplies" that meet those demands. Sort both; walk both with two pointers. If the current supply meets the current demand, match and advance both. If not, the supply is too small for all future demands too — discard it.

In systems contexts: assigning tasks to threads where tasks have minimum CPU requirements and threads have fixed capacities; assigning memory requests to memory blocks where requests have minimum size and blocks have fixed size; matching packets to QoS queues where packets have minimum bandwidth requirements.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 455 — Assign Cookiescanonical form; maximize satisfied children
  • LeetCode 2410 — Maximum Matching of Players With Trainerssame two-sorted-array matching structure
  • LeetCode 826 — Most Profit Assigning Workprofit-maximizing variant; sort by difficulty, match with worker ability
  • Thread-pool task schedulingtasks have min CPU need; workers have fixed CPU; maximize tasks assigned
  • Memory block allocatorrequests have min size; blocks have exact size; first-fit or best-fit allocation is this pattern
  • QoS packet schedulingpackets have min bandwidth need; queues have fixed bandwidth; assign queues to maximize served packets
③ Building the Logic from Scratch

Exchange argument: Suppose an optimal solution assigns a big cookie to a small-greed child. If we swap in the smallest sufficient cookie instead, we free up the big cookie for a greedier child (or discard it if unused). The swap never decreases the count of satisfied children. Therefore sorting and matching smallest-to-smallest is optimal.

g = [1, 2, 3]    (greed, sorted ascending)
s = [1, 1, 2]   (cookies, sorted ascending)

child=0(g=1), cookie=0(s=1): 1>=1 → match  child→1, cookie→1
child=1(g=2), cookie=1(s=1): 1<2  → skip   cookie→2
child=1(g=2), cookie=2(s=2): 2>=2 → match  child→2, cookie→3 (exhausted)
Loop ends.

return 2 ✓

Aha moment: the cookie pointer advances unconditionally — even if the cookie is too small it's useless for all future (greedier) children, so you must discard it. Only the child pointer advances on a successful match.

Pseudocode:

sort g ascending, sort s ascending
child = 0, cookie = 0
while child < len(g) and cookie < len(s):
    if s[cookie] >= g[child]:
        child++       // satisfied
    cookie++          // always advance cookie
return child
④ Full Code Walkthrough
int cmp_asc(const void *a, const void *b) {
    int x = *(const int *)a, y = *(const int *)b;
    if (x < y) return -1; if (x > y) return 1; return 0;
}

/* Maximum satisfied children. g[gn]=greed factors, s[sn]=cookie sizes. */
int find_content_children(int *g, int gn, int *s, int sn) {
    qsort(g, gn, sizeof(int), cmp_asc);
    qsort(s, sn, sizeof(int), cmp_asc);

    int child  = 0;   /* next unsatisfied child (greed ascending) */
    int cookie = 0;   /* next unused cookie (size ascending) */

    while (child < gn && cookie < sn) {
        if (s[cookie] >= g[child])   /* cookie is large enough — satisfy this child */
            child++;
        cookie++;   /* cookie is consumed whether or not it matched — too small for all future children if it didn't match */
    }
    return child;
}
⑤ Dry Run
g=[1,2,3], s=[1,1] — fewer cookies than children child | g[child] | cookie | s[cookie] | match? | note -------+----------+--------+-----------+--------+----- 0 | 1 | 0 | 1 | yes | child→1, cookie→1 1 | 2 | 1 | 1 | no | cookie→2 (exhausted) return 1 ✓ g=[1,2], s=[3] — one big cookie for two children child | g[child] | cookie | s[cookie] | match? -------+----------+--------+-----------+------- 0 | 1 | 0 | 3 | yes → child→1, cookie→1 (exhausted) return 1 ✓ (can't give one cookie to two children)
⑥ Gotcha
⚠ Wrong: only advancing cookie on a failed match
/* WRONG */
while (child < gn && cookie < sn) {
    if (s[cookie] >= g[child]) {
        child++;
        cookie++;   // only advance cookie on success
    } else {
        cookie++;   // also here — but wait, this is actually the same...
    }
}
/* Actually both branches advance cookie, so this is correct.
   The WRONG version is when you forget to advance cookie at all
   on failure: */
if (s[cookie] >= g[child]) { child++; cookie++; }
/* ← cookie never advances on failure → infinite loop */

What breaks: If you only advance cookie when the match succeeds, a cookie that's too small stays in place forever — infinite loop.

/* CORRECT: cookie always advances */
if (s[cookie] >= g[child]) child++;
cookie++;   /* unconditional — outside the if */

Rule: The resource (cookie) pointer always advances. The demand (child) pointer only advances on a successful match.

⑦ Confidence Check

1. Why is sorting both arrays a prerequisite — what goes wrong if either array is unsorted?

2. If a cookie is too small to satisfy the current child, why is it guaranteed to also be too small for every subsequent child?

3. This problem asks to maximize satisfied children. Would the same algorithm still work if instead you wanted to maximize the total "happiness" (some function of matched pairs)?

⑧ Trigger
When you see "greedily match two sorted arrays where one set has minimum requirements and the other has fixed capacities" → sort both, use two pointers — always advance the resource pointer, only advance the demand pointer on a successful match.
Also fires for: "assign tasks to workers by capability" · "match requests to memory blocks" · "maximize feasible pairings from two sets"
Pattern 34 Matrix: Spiral Order Traversal
① Background

Given an m×n matrix, output all elements in spiral order: traverse the top row left-to-right, then the right column top-to-bottom, then the bottom row right-to-left, then the left column bottom-to-top — then repeat for the inner ring, and so on until all elements are visited.

A direction-cycling approach (right→down→left→up→right…) with a visited grid works but requires O(mn) extra memory for the visited array. The boundary-shrink approach uses only four integer variables and no extra memory. After each edge is consumed, that boundary collapses inward, so the same traversal logic naturally avoids re-visiting.

The subtle correctness requirement is guarding the bottom-row and left-column traversals. After consuming the top row and right column in a pass, you may have reduced the matrix to zero remaining rows (top > bottom) or zero remaining columns (left > right). Without checking, you'd traverse a row or column that no longer exists — or worse, re-traverse elements in reverse, producing duplicates.

Spiral traversal appears in image processing (reading a convolution kernel from outside in), radar sweep algorithms, cache-warming for 2D data structures, and display buffer fills for embedded devices that write pixels along a snake path to maximize cache locality.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 54 — Spiral Matrixcanonical read form; output elements in spiral order
  • LeetCode 59 — Spiral Matrix IIwrite form; fill a matrix with 1..n² in spiral order
  • Image kernel applicationread a convolution kernel from center outward or outside inward for progressive refinement
  • Radar sweep / sonar scansample a 2D field ring by ring from perimeter to center
  • Embedded display buffer fillwrite pixels in a spiral path to maximize DRAM row-buffer hits for certain memory layouts
  • Layer-peeling matrix algorithmsany algorithm that processes a matrix from outside in — edge detection, border zeroing
③ Building the Logic from Scratch

Think of peeling an onion: each iteration of the outer while-loop peels one ring. A ring has four edges: top, right, bottom, left. After each edge is traversed, the corresponding boundary shrinks inward by one. The outer loop exits when the boundaries cross.

matrix = [[1, 2, 3],
          [4, 5, 6],
          [7, 8, 9]]

Initial boundaries: top=0, bottom=2, left=0, right=2

Pass 1:
  Top row   (row 0, cols 0→2): 1, 2, 3  → top=1
  Right col (col 2, rows 1→2): 6, 9     → right=1
  Bottom row (row 2, cols 1→0): 8, 7    → bottom=1   (guard: top=1<=bottom=2 ✓)
  Left col  (col 0, rows 1→1): 4        → left=1     (guard: left=0<=right=1 ✓)

Pass 2: top=1, bottom=1, left=1, right=1
  Top row (row 1, col 1): 5  → top=2
  top(2) > bottom(1) → stop

Spiral: [1, 2, 3, 6, 9, 8, 7, 4, 5] ✓

Aha moment: the bottom-row and left-column edges need existence guards. After consuming the top row and right column, you might have used up all the rows or columns. Without checking, you revisit elements in reverse.

Pseudocode:

top=0, bottom=rows-1, left=0, right=cols-1
while top <= bottom and left <= right:
    traverse row[top] left→right;  top++
    traverse col[right] top→bottom; right--
    if top <= bottom: traverse row[bottom] right→left; bottom--
    if left <= right: traverse col[left] bottom→top; left++
④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Spiral order traversal. result[] pre-allocated to rows*cols elements. */
void spiral_order(int matrix[][100], int rows, int cols, int *result) {
    int top    = 0, bottom = rows - 1;
    int left   = 0, right  = cols - 1;
    int k = 0;

    while (top <= bottom && left <= right) {
        for (int c = left;   c <= right;  c++) result[k++] = matrix[top][c];
        top++;    /* top ring edge consumed */

        for (int r = top;    r <= bottom; r++) result[k++] = matrix[r][right];
        right--;  /* right ring edge consumed */

        if (top <= bottom) {   /* guard: at least one row still remains */
            for (int c = right; c >= left; c--) result[k++] = matrix[bottom][c];
            bottom--;
        }

        if (left <= right) {   /* guard: at least one column still remains */
            for (int r = bottom; r >= top; r--) result[k++] = matrix[r][left];
            left++;
        }
    }
}
⑤ Dry Run
3×3: [[1,2,3],[4,5,6],[7,8,9]] Pass 1: top=0,bot=2,left=0,right=2 Top row → 1,2,3 top→1 Right col → 6,9 right→1 guard ok: bottom row → 8,7 bot→1 guard ok: left col → 4 left→1 Pass 2: top=1,bot=1,left=1,right=1 Top row → 5 top→2 Right col (top=2 > bot=1): no elements guard: top(2) > bot(1) → skip bottom row guard: left(1) <= right(1) → left col (bot=0..top=2 reversed): no elements (bot<top) outer: top(2) > bot(1) → exit Result: [1,2,3,6,9,8,7,4,5] ✓ 1×3: [[1,2,3]] top=0,bot=0,left=0,right=2 Top row → 1,2,3 top→1 Right col (top=1>bot=0): no elements guard: top(1)>bot(0) → skip guard: left(0)<=right(2) → left col (bot=0..top=1 reversed): no elements outer: top>bot → exit Result: [1,2,3] ✓
⑥ Gotcha
⚠ Wrong: no guard on the bottom-row and left-column traversals
/* WRONG: missing guards */
for (int c = right; c >= left; c--)  result[k++] = matrix[bottom][c];
bottom--;
for (int r = bottom; r >= top; r--)  result[k++] = matrix[r][left];
left++;

What breaks: For a 1×3 matrix [[1,2,3]], after the top-row pass top=1 > bottom=0. Without the guard, the bottom-row loop runs with bottom=0, traversing elements already collected in reverse — producing duplicates [1,2,3,3,2,1].

/* CORRECT */
if (top <= bottom) {
    for (int c = right; c >= left; c--) result[k++] = matrix[bottom][c];
    bottom--;
}
if (left <= right) {
    for (int r = bottom; r >= top; r--) result[k++] = matrix[r][left];
    left++;
}

Rule: After advancing top and retreating right, always re-check whether rows and columns still remain before the backward traversals.

⑦ Confidence Check

1. Why does the outer while loop need both conditions top <= bottom AND left <= right rather than just one of them?

2. After the top row is traversed and top++ executes, the right-column loop uses the updated top. Why is this correct and not an off-by-one error?

3. For a single-row matrix, which of the four edge traversals actually runs, and which are skipped by the guards?

⑧ Trigger
When you see "traverse a matrix layer by layer from outside in" → use four shrinking boundaries; guard the backward traversals against already-consumed rows/columns.
Also fires for: "fill a matrix in spiral order" · "process a 2D grid ring by ring" · "peel a matrix like an onion"
Pattern 35 Matrix: Set Row and Column to Zero
① Background

Given an m×n matrix, if any element is 0, set its entire row and column to 0. The operation must reflect the original zeros, not any zeros introduced by the operation itself.

The naive single-pass approach fails because writing zeros as you go creates new zeros that look like original zeros on subsequent reads. This "cascade" effect turns many more rows and columns to zero than intended. The fix is to separate detection from application: first record which rows and columns originally contain zeros, then apply the zeroing using only that recorded information.

The O(m+n) space version uses two boolean arrays. The O(1) space version is more subtle: use the first row and first column of the matrix itself as the flag storage, but you must separately track whether the first row and first column themselves originally contained zeros before you repurpose them as flags — otherwise you'll corrupt the answer for row 0 and column 0.

This "mark-then-apply" pattern appears everywhere data must be transformed based on its original state: image morphological operations (erosion/dilation), cellular automaton updates (Conway's Game of Life), adjacency matrix transformations, and network connectivity updates where simultaneous state changes are required.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 73 — Set Matrix Zeroescanonical form; O(1) space variant uses first row/col as flags
  • LeetCode 289 — Game of Lifesame mark-then-apply discipline; encode next state in same cell without losing current state
  • Image erosion/dilationnew pixel value depends on original neighbors — must not read modified values during the pass
  • Graph adjacency matrix updatepropagating reachability: mark reachable nodes first, then update edges simultaneously
  • Cellular automaton stepeach cell's next state depends on its original neighbors — single-pass modification causes incorrect cascades
  • Sensor fault maskingzero out entire row/column of a reading matrix when a fault sensor is detected — detection pass first
③ Building the Logic from Scratch

Why single-pass fails: Suppose matrix[1][1] = 0. You zero row 1. Now matrix[1][0] = 0. In the next iteration you see that new zero and zero row 1 and column 0 — but column 0 had no original zero. Cascade.

matrix = [[1, 2, 3],
          [4, 0, 6],
          [7, 8, 9]]

WRONG (single pass):
  See (1,1)=0 → zero row 1: [[1,2,3],[0,0,0],[7,8,9]]
  See (1,0)=0 (new!) → zero col 0: [[0,2,3],[0,0,0],[0,8,9]]  ← WRONG

CORRECT (mark first):
  Pass 1: see (1,1)=0 → mark row_flag[1]=1, col_flag[1]=1
  Pass 2: zero row 1  → [[1,2,3],[0,0,0],[7,8,9]]
  Pass 3: zero col 1  → [[1,0,3],[0,0,0],[7,0,9]] ✓

Aha moment: the two-pass structure isn't about performance — it's about correctness. You can't trust values you've written yourself during detection.

Pseudocode:

row_flags[rows] = all 0
col_flags[cols] = all 0

for r, c: if matrix[r][c] == 0: row_flags[r]=1, col_flags[c]=1
for r: if row_flags[r]: zero entire row r
for c: if col_flags[c]: zero entire col c
④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Set row and col to 0 for every original 0 cell. O(m+n) space. */
void set_zeros(int matrix[][100], int rows, int cols) {
    int row_flag[100] = {0};
    int col_flag[100] = {0};

    for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++)       /* pass 1: detect original zeros */
        for (int c = 0; c < cols; c++)
            if (matrix[r][c] == 0) {
                row_flag[r] = 1;
                col_flag[c] = 1;
            }

    for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++)       /* pass 2: apply row zeroing */
        if (row_flag[r])
            for (int c = 0; c < cols; c++) matrix[r][c] = 0;

    for (int c = 0; c < cols; c++)       /* pass 3: apply column zeroing */
        if (col_flag[c])
            for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++) matrix[r][c] = 0;
}
⑤ Dry Run
matrix = [[1,1,1],[1,0,1],[1,1,1]] Pass 1: (1,1)=0 → row_flag[1]=1, col_flag[1]=1 Pass 2: row 1 → [[1,1,1],[0,0,0],[1,1,1]] Pass 3: col 1 → [[1,0,1],[0,0,0],[1,0,1]] ✓ Multiple zeros: [[0,1,2],[3,4,0],[5,6,7]] Pass 1: (0,0)=0 → row_flag[0]=1, col_flag[0]=1 (1,2)=0 → row_flag[1]=1, col_flag[2]=1 Pass 2: zero rows 0,1 → [[0,0,0],[0,0,0],[5,6,7]] Pass 3: zero cols 0,2 → [[0,0,0],[0,0,0],[0,6,0]] ✓
⑥ Gotcha
⚠ Wrong: zeroing during the detection pass (cascade)
/* WRONG: single-pass zeroing */
for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++)
    for (int c = 0; c < cols; c++)
        if (matrix[r][c] == 0) {
            for (int k = 0; k < cols; k++) matrix[r][k] = 0;  /* WRONG */
            for (int k = 0; k < rows; k++) matrix[k][c] = 0;  /* WRONG */
        }

What breaks: Zeros written during the first iteration are read as original zeros in later iterations, cascading the zeroing to rows and columns that had no original zeros.

/* CORRECT: separate detection from application */
/* pass 1: record flags only */
if (matrix[r][c] == 0) { row_flag[r] = 1; col_flag[c] = 1; }
/* pass 2+3: apply flags */

Rule: Never read a value you've already written in a transformation that depends on original values — mark first, apply second.

⑦ Confidence Check

1. Why can't you combine passes 2 and 3 into a single pass that zeroes both the row and column for each flagged cell?

2. The O(1) space variant uses the first row and column as flag storage. What extra state do you need to track, and why?

3. If two original zeros share the same row, does the algorithm zero that row twice? Is that a problem?

⑧ Trigger
When you see "transform a matrix based on its original values" → never read and write in the same pass; mark which rows/columns to transform in a first pass, apply in a second.
Also fires for: "Game of Life / cellular automaton step" · "image erosion or dilation" · "cascade-free bulk updates to a 2D structure"
Pattern 36 Matrix: Rotate 90 Degrees In-Place
① Background

Rotate an n×n matrix 90 degrees clockwise without using an extra matrix. The naive approach copies elements to a fresh array — O(n²) extra space. The in-place trick decomposes the rotation into two simpler operations that each run in-place: transpose, then reverse each row.

Why does this work? A 90° clockwise rotation maps element at (r, c) to position (c, n-1-r). Transposing maps (r, c) → (c, r). Then reversing each row maps (c, r) → (c, n-1-r). Combining: (r, c) → (c, n-1-r). Exactly correct.

Counter-clockwise 90° is: transpose then reverse each column (or equivalently: reverse each row first, then transpose). 180° is: reverse each row and each column independently. These identities are worth memorizing — they collapse an apparently complex geometric operation to two linear passes.

In-place matrix rotation appears in image processing (rotating a frame buffer without allocating a second buffer), display drivers (portrait/landscape mode flip on memory-constrained hardware), and game engines that rotate tile maps or sprite sheets in place during asset loading.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 48 — Rotate Imagecanonical form; clockwise 90° in-place on an n×n matrix
  • LeetCode 1886 — Matrix Rotation Checkapply rotation and compare; uses this decomposition as a subroutine
  • Display driver portrait/landscape fliprotate framebuffer in place when changing orientation on embedded hardware
  • Game engine tile map rotationrotate a 2D tile grid in place during level load without a second buffer
  • Image processing kernel rotationrotate a convolution kernel in place for oriented-filter banks
