Aha moment: always store indices in the stack, not values. You need the index to write to result[j], but you use arr[j] for the comparison. Storing values loses the ability to write to the correct output slot.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* result[i] = first arr[j] > arr[i] for j > i, or -1 if none. */
void next_greater(int *arr, int n, int *result) {
    int stack[n];   /* indices of elements waiting for their next-greater */
    int top = 0;

    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) result[i] = -1;   /* default: no next greater found */

    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        /* arr[i] is the next-greater for all waiting elements smaller than it */
        while (top > 0 && arr[stack[top-1]] < arr[i])
            result[stack[--top]] = arr[i];   /* record answer for popped index */
        stack[top++] = i;   /* i is now waiting for its next-greater */
    }
    /* elements still in stack have no next-greater — result stays -1 */
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr=[4,1,2,3] i | arr[i] | pops | stack after | result so far ---+--------+--------------------+-----------------+-------------- 0 | 4 | none | [0] | [-1,-1,-1,-1] 1 | 1 | none (1<4) | [0,1] | [-1,-1,-1,-1] 2 | 2 | pop 1 (1<2)→r[1]=2| [0,2] | [-1, 2,-1,-1] 3 | 3 | pop 2 (2<3)→r[2]=3| [0,3] | [-1, 2, 3,-1] End: stack=[0,3] → result[0]=-1, result[3]=-1 Final: [-1, 2, 3, -1] ✓ arr=[1,3,2,4] — check multiple pops at one step i=3: arr[3]=4. stack=[0(1),1(3),2(2)]. pop 2 (2<4) → result[2]=4 pop 1 (3<4) → result[1]=4 pop 0 (1<4) → result[0]=4 stack empty. push 3. result=[4,4,4,-1] ✓
⑥ Gotcha
⚠ Wrong: storing values instead of indices in the stack
/* WRONG */
stack[top++] = arr[i];   /* store the value */
while (top > 0 && stack[top-1] < arr[i]) {
    int val = stack[--top];
    /* which result index do we update? we lost the original index! */
}

What breaks: You can compare arr[i] against stored values, but when you pop you need to write to result[original_index] — and you've discarded the index.

/* CORRECT: store indices, use arr[index] for comparison */
stack[top++] = i;
while (top > 0 && arr[stack[top-1]] < arr[i])
    result[stack[--top]] = arr[i];   /* stack holds index, use it to write result */

Rule: Monotonic stacks almost always store indices — you need the index for output, and arr[index] for the comparison predicate.

⑦ Confidence Check

1. Why is the stack guaranteed to be in monotonically decreasing order of values at all times?

2. What is the total number of push and pop operations across the entire algorithm, and why does this give O(n) time?

3. How would you adapt this for the circular variant (LeetCode 503), where the array wraps around?

⑧ Trigger
When you see "first greater/smaller element to the right for each position" → monotonic stack of indices; pop when current breaks monotonicity; remaining stack entries get -1.
Also fires for: "stock span (nearest greater to the left)" · "building visibility" · "largest rectangle histogram (nearest shorter bar)"
Pattern 50 Monotonic Stack: Daily Temperatures
① Background

Given a list of daily temperatures, for each day find how many days you must wait until a warmer day arrives. If no warmer day ever comes, the answer is 0. The result is an array of waiting times, not the temperatures themselves.

This is Next Greater Element in disguise, with one difference: instead of recording the value of the next greater element, record the distance to it. The algorithm is identical — monotonic decreasing stack of indices. When day i resolves day j's wait (because temps[i] > temps[j]), store result[j] = i − j.

The key discipline: store indices in the stack, not temperatures. You need the index to compute the gap (i − j) and to write to result[j]. Temperatures are read via temps[index]. Days remaining in the stack at the end had no warmer successor — result[j] stays 0 for them.

Daily Temperatures is often introduced alongside Next Greater Element to show that the same stack template handles "value of next greater" and "distance to next greater" equally well — the only change is what you write to the result array when popping.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 739 — Daily Temperaturescanonical form; how many days until warmer; result = index gap
  • LeetCode 901 — Online Stock Spaninverted: how many consecutive prior days were ≤ today's price — monotonic non-decreasing stack
  • LeetCode 496 — Next Greater Element Isister problem; records value instead of gap
  • Traffic signal wait timefor each car queue length reading, how many time steps until a shorter queue appears
  • CPU temperature throttlingfor each second, how many seconds until temperature drops below a threshold — inverted monotonic stack
  • Network latency spike analysisfor each latency reading, how many samples until a lower-latency sample is observed
③ Building the Logic from Scratch

Same stack, different output: The algorithm is identical to Next Greater Element. The only change is the line that fills the result array. Instead of result[j] = arr[i], write result[j] = i - j.

temps = [73, 74, 75, 71, 69, 72, 76, 73]
idx:      0   1   2   3   4   5   6   7

i=0: push 0. stack=[0(73)]
i=1: 74>73 → pop 0: result[0]=1-0=1. push 1. stack=[1(74)]
i=2: 75>74 → pop 1: result[1]=2-1=1. push 2. stack=[2(75)]
i=3: 71<75 → push 3. stack=[2(75),3(71)]
i=4: 69<71 → push 4. stack=[2(75),3(71),4(69)]
i=5: 72>69 → pop 4: result[4]=5-4=1.
     72>71 → pop 3: result[3]=5-3=2.
     72<75 → push 5. stack=[2(75),5(72)]
i=6: 76>72 → pop 5: result[5]=6-5=1.
     76>75 → pop 2: result[2]=6-2=4.
     push 6. stack=[6(76)]
i=7: 73<76 → push 7. stack=[6(76),7(73)]

End: stack=[6,7] → result[6]=0, result[7]=0
result = [1,1,4,2,1,1,0,0] ✓

Aha moment: result[j] = 0 is the "no warmer day" sentinel, initialized before the loop. Days left in the stack at the end automatically get 0 — no post-loop cleanup needed.

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* result[i] = days until a warmer temperature; 0 if none. */
void daily_temperatures(int *temps, int n, int *result) {
    int stack[n];   /* indices of days still waiting for a warmer day */
    int top = 0;

    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) result[i] = 0;   /* 0 = no warmer day found */

    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        while (top > 0 && temps[stack[top-1]] < temps[i]) {
            int j = stack[--top];
            result[j] = i - j;   /* i is the warmer day; waited i-j days */
        }
        stack[top++] = i;
    }
    /* days still in stack: result stays 0 — no warmer day ever came */
}
⑤ Dry Run
temps=[73,74,75,71,69,72,76,73] i | temps[i] | pops (result updates) | stack ---+----------+---------------------------------+------- 0 | 73 | none | [0] 1 | 74 | pop 0: r[0]=1 | [1] 2 | 75 | pop 1: r[1]=1 | [2] 3 | 71 | none | [2,3] 4 | 69 | none | [2,3,4] 5 | 72 | pop 4: r[4]=1; pop 3: r[3]=2 | [2,5] 6 | 76 | pop 5: r[5]=1; pop 2: r[2]=4 | [6] 7 | 73 | none (73<76) | [6,7] result = [1,1,4,2,1,1,0,0] ✓
⑥ Gotcha
⚠ Wrong: storing the warmer temperature value instead of the day count
/* WRONG */
int j = stack[--top];
result[j] = temps[i];   /* stores the warmer TEMPERATURE, not the NUMBER OF DAYS */

What breaks: The problem asks how many days to wait, not what the temperature will be. Storing temps[i] gives the temperature on the warmer day, which is a different and incorrect output.

/* CORRECT */
result[j] = i - j;   /* the gap between day j and the warmer day i */

Rule: Daily Temperatures and Next Greater Element use the same stack; the only difference is what you write: i − j for the wait time, arr[i] for the next-greater value.

⑦ Confidence Check

1. Why does initializing result to 0 before the loop handle the "no warmer day" case correctly, rather than needing a post-loop check?

2. If the temperatures are strictly decreasing (e.g., [5,4,3,2,1]), trace the algorithm: how many pushes happen, how many pops, and what is the final result?

3. How would you adapt this for "how many days until a cooler temperature?" — what changes in the comparison and the default value?

⑧ Trigger
When you see "how many steps until the next larger value" → monotonic stack of indices; pop when condition met; result[j] = i − j (the gap), not the value.
Also fires for: "next day with higher stock price" · "next reading above threshold" · "steps until constraint is satisfied"
Pattern 51 Bit Manipulation: Single Number (XOR Trick)
① Background

Every number in an array appears exactly twice, except one which appears exactly once. Find the unique number in O(n) time and O(1) space. A hash set works but uses O(n) space. Sorting works but is O(n log n). XOR does it in a single pass with no extra memory.

XOR has three properties that make this work: (1) X ^ X = 0 — a value XORed with itself vanishes; (2) X ^ 0 = X — XOR with zero is identity; (3) XOR is commutative and associative — order doesn't matter. Therefore XOR-ing all elements causes all paired values to cancel to 0, leaving only the unpaired value.

The gotcha: XOR only cancels when a value appears an even number of times. If "duplicates appear exactly twice" is the problem guarantee, XOR is perfect. If duplicates appear three times (Single Number II), you need a bit-counting approach (count each bit position modulo 3). If there are two unpaired values (Single Number III), XOR gives you their XOR — then use the rightmost set bit to split them into two groups.

XOR-based tricks appear in: error detection (parity bits), RAID-5 parity recovery (if one disk fails, XOR all others to reconstruct), Gray code generation, hash functions for sets where membership toggles (add/remove by XOR), and network packet checksum validation.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 136 — Single Numbercanonical form; XOR all elements, unique survives
  • LeetCode 137 — Single Number IIappears 3× except one; count each bit modulo 3
  • LeetCode 260 — Single Number IIItwo unpaired values; XOR then split by rightmost differing bit
  • RAID-5 parity recoveryXOR all surviving disk blocks to reconstruct the lost block — same cancellation property
  • Network parity error detectionXOR all bytes in a packet; if parity byte XOR gives 0, no single-bit error
  • Toggle-set membershiprepresent a set where insert/delete both XOR the element in — membership test by XOR accumulation
③ Building the Logic from Scratch

Why XOR works: XOR is associative and commutative, so the order of operations doesn't matter. Group all pairs together mentally: each pair XORs to 0. The single element XORs with all the zeros and survives as itself.

arr = [4, 1, 2, 1, 2]

Mental regrouping (commutativity/associativity):
= 4 ^ (1^1) ^ (2^2)
= 4 ^ 0 ^ 0
= 4 ✓

Step-by-step accumulation:
  result = 0
  0 ^ 4 = 4
  4 ^ 1 = 5  (0100 ^ 0001 = 0101)
  5 ^ 2 = 7  (0101 ^ 0010 = 0111)
  7 ^ 1 = 6  (0111 ^ 0001 = 0110)
  6 ^ 2 = 4  (0110 ^ 0010 = 0100) ✓

Single Number III (two unpaired values a and b):

XOR all → xor_all = a ^ b (≠ 0 since a ≠ b)
Pick any set bit in xor_all: that bit differs between a and b.
Split array into two groups by that bit:
  group 1 (bit set):   XOR all → gives a (or b)
  group 2 (bit clear): XOR all → gives b (or a)

Aha moment: x & (-x) isolates the rightmost set bit of x. This is the standard C idiom for "find a bit where two values differ."

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Find the element appearing once (all others appear twice). O(n) O(1). */
int single_number(int *arr, int n) {
    int result = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        result ^= arr[i];   /* pairs cancel; the lone value survives */
    return result;
}

/* Find two elements each appearing once (all others appear twice). */
void single_number_iii(int *arr, int n, int *a, int *b) {
    int xor_all = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) xor_all ^= arr[i];   /* xor_all = a ^ b */

    int diff = xor_all & (-xor_all);   /* rightmost bit where a and b differ */

    *a = 0; *b = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (arr[i] & diff) *a ^= arr[i];   /* group with bit set: XOR → a */
        else                *b ^= arr[i];   /* group with bit clear: XOR → b */
    }
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr=[4,1,2,1,2] — basic single number result = 0^4^1^2^1^2 = 4^(1^1)^(2^2) = 4^0^0 = 4 ✓ arr=[1,2,1,3,2,5] — two unpaired values (3 and 5) xor_all = 1^2^1^3^2^5 = (1^1)^(2^2)^3^5 = 3^5 = 6 (110 in binary) diff = 6 & (-6) = 6 & ...11111010 = 010 = 2 (rightmost set bit) Group by bit 1 (diff=2): arr[i] & 2: 1 (01): clear → *b ^= 1 2 (10): set → *a ^= 2 1 (01): clear → *b ^= 1 (1^1=0) 3 (11): set → *a ^= 3 (2^3=1) 2 (10): set → *a ^= 2 (1^2=3) 5 (101): clear→ *b ^= 5 (0^5=5) *a = 3, *b = 5 ✓
⑥ Gotcha
⚠ Wrong: assuming XOR works when elements appear 3 times
/* WRONG for Single Number II: arr=[2,2,3,2], answer=3 */
int result = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) result ^= arr[i];
/* result = 2^2^3^2 = (2^2)^3^2 = 0^3^2 = 1 ≠ 3 — WRONG */

What breaks: XOR cancels even occurrences. Three occurrences of 2 leave one 2 uncancel­led: 2^2^2 = 2. The result includes the survivor, giving wrong output.

/* CORRECT for "appears 3×, find the 1×": count each bit modulo 3 */
int ones = 0, twos = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    ones = (ones ^ arr[i]) & ~twos;
    twos = (twos ^ arr[i]) & ~ones;
}
return ones;   /* bits that appeared 1 mod 3 times */

Rule: XOR cancels even counts. For odd-count scenarios other than 1-or-2, use bit-counting modulo the repetition count.

⑦ Confidence Check

1. Why does the order of elements in the array not matter for the XOR accumulation?

2. In Single Number III, why does isolating the rightmost set bit of (a ^ b) guarantee that a and b land in different groups?

3. If the problem said "all elements appear exactly 3 times except one that appears once," why would XOR fail, and what would you do instead?

⑧ Trigger
When you see "find the element that appears an odd number of times (all others even)" → XOR all elements; pairs cancel to 0; the survivor is the answer.
Also fires for: "RAID parity recovery" · "parity bit error detection" · "two unpaired values (XOR + rightmost-bit split)"
Pattern 52 Bit Manipulation: Missing Number
① Background

Given an array of n distinct integers in the range [0, n], find the one missing value. The array has n elements but the range [0, n] has n+1 values — exactly one is absent. Two O(n) O(1) approaches exist: the Gauss sum approach and the XOR approach.

Sum approach: The expected sum of 0+1+2+...+n is n*(n+1)/2 (Gauss formula). Subtract the actual array sum. The difference is the missing number. Straightforward, but risks integer overflow for large n (n*(n+1)/2 overflows 32-bit int when n ≈ 65536).

XOR approach: XOR all indices 0..n and all array elements. Since paired values cancel (index k XOR element k = 0 for all present values k), only the missing index's XOR survives unmatched. This approach is overflow-safe because XOR is a bitwise operation with no carry.

The sum approach is simpler to reason about. The XOR approach is the right choice when overflow is a concern or when you want to practice the XOR cancellation mental model that underpins Single Number and many other bit-manipulation problems.

② Where You See This
  • LeetCode 268 — Missing Numbercanonical form; sum or XOR approach
  • LeetCode 645 — Set Mismatchone number appears twice and one is missing — find both with sum and sum-of-squares
  • LeetCode 41 — First Missing Positiveharder variant: missing positive in arbitrary range — needs cyclic sort (P47)
  • Memory page ID validationgiven n page IDs that should cover [0..n], find the gap — sum comparison of expected vs actual
  • Sequence integrity checkverify a sequence of log event IDs contains no gaps — compare expected XOR with actual XOR
  • UART packet sequence number gap detectionfind the missing sequence number in a received packet window using sum arithmetic
③ Building the Logic from Scratch

Sum approach: Range [0..n] should sum to n*(n+1)/2. Actual sum is what we compute. Their difference is exactly the missing value.

arr = [3, 0, 1],  n = 3
Expected sum = 3*4/2 = 6
Actual sum   = 3+0+1 = 4
Missing      = 6 - 4 = 2 ✓

XOR approach: Think of it as two arrays: [0,1,2,...,n] and arr[0..n-1]. XOR all elements of both. Every index k that is present in arr pairs with index k — they XOR to 0. Only the missing index has no match.

arr = [3, 0, 1],  n = 3
XOR 0..3:     0^1^2^3 = 0
XOR arr:      3^0^1   = 2
XOR both:     0^2     = 2 ✓

Implementation: start with result=n, then XOR each i ^ arr[i]:
  result = 3
  i=0: result ^= 0 ^ 3 = 3^0^3 = 0
  i=1: result ^= 1 ^ 0 = 0^1^0 = 1
  i=2: result ^= 2 ^ 1 = 1^2^1 = 2 ✓

Aha moment: XOR of indices [0..n] XOR elements present = XOR of the missing index (all others cancel). Initializing result=n handles the fact that the index range is [0..n] but arr only has n elements (0..n-1).

④ Full Code Walkthrough
/* Find missing number in arr[0..n-1] containing n distinct values from [0, n]. */

/* Sum approach: O(n) time, O(1) space, risk of overflow for large n */
int missing_number_sum(int *arr, int n) {
    long long expected = (long long)n * (n + 1) / 2;   /* cast to avoid overflow */
    long long actual   = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) actual += arr[i];
    return (int)(expected - actual);
}

/* XOR approach: O(n) time, O(1) space, overflow-safe */
int missing_number_xor(int *arr, int n) {
    int result = n;            /* start with n to include the index n in the XOR */
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        result ^= i ^ arr[i]; /* each present value k: index k ^ element k = 0 */
    return result;             /* only the missing index survives unmatched */
}
⑤ Dry Run
arr=[3,0,1], n=3 — sum approach expected = 3*4/2 = 6 actual = 3+0+1 = 4 return 6-4 = 2 ✓ arr=[3,0,1], n=3 — XOR approach result = 3 (start with n) i=0: result ^= 0 ^ arr[0] = result ^= 0^3 = 3^0^3 = 0 i=1: result ^= 1 ^ arr[1] = 0 ^= 1^0 = 0^1 = 1 i=2: result ^= 2 ^ arr[2] = 1 ^= 2^1 = 1^3 = 2 return 2 ✓ Edge: arr=[], n=0 — empty array, missing is 0 Sum: expected=0, actual=0, return 0 ✓ XOR: result=0, loop doesn't run, return 0 ✓
⑥ Gotcha
⚠ Wrong: integer overflow in the sum approach with int
/* WRONG for large n */
int expected = n * (n + 1) / 2;   /* overflows when n*(n+1) > INT_MAX ≈ 2.1 billion */
/* n=65536: 65536*65537 = 4,295,032,832 > 2,147,483,647 → overflow, wrong answer */

What breaks: For n ≈ 65536, n*(n+1) exceeds 32-bit int range. The multiplication wraps around before the division, giving a wrong expected sum.

/* CORRECT: cast to long long before multiplication */
long long expected = (long long)n * (n + 1) / 2;

Rule: When computing n*(n+1)/2 in C, cast n to long long before multiplying. Alternatively, use the XOR approach which has no addition and therefore no overflow risk.

⑦ Confidence Check

1. In the XOR approach, why does initializing result=n (rather than 0) correctly account for the full index range [0..n]?

2. If the array had two missing numbers instead of one, could the sum approach still find both? What additional information would you need?

3. The XOR approach XORs each index i and element arr[i] together. What if arr[i] == i for some i — does XOR still work correctly?

⑧ Trigger
When you see "find the one missing value in [0..n]" → sum: expected − actual (cast to long long); XOR: start with n, XOR each i^arr[i] — missing index survives unmatched.
Also fires for: "verify sequence completeness" · "find gap in a range of IDs" · "one duplicate + one missing (sum + sum-of-squares)"
Pattern 53 Divide & Conquer: Count Inversions (Merge Sort)
① Story First
An inversion is a pair (i,j) where i < j but arr[i] > arr[j] — out of order. Count them all in O(n log n) using merge sort. During the merge step, whenever we pick from the right half, all remaining left-half elements are larger than it — they ALL form inversions with this right element.
② The Insight
During merge of two sorted halves [L] and [R]: when we pick R[j] because L[i] > R[j], all of L[i..end] are also > R[j] (L is sorted). So we add (mid - i) inversions at once — an O(1) count per element.
⑤ Annotated Code
long long merge_count_inv(int *arr, int *tmp, int lo, int hi) {
    if (hi - lo <= 1) return 0;   /* single element: no inversions */
    int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;

    long long count = merge_count_inv(arr, tmp, lo,  mid)
                    + merge_count_inv(arr, tmp, mid, hi);

    int i = lo, j = mid, k = lo;
    while (i < mid && j < hi) {
        if (arr[i] <= arr[j]) {
            tmp[k++] = arr[i++];   /* left element is smaller: no inversion */
        } else {
            /* arr[j] < arr[i], and all of arr[i..mid-1] > arr[j] (left is sorted) */
            count  += (mid - i);   /* all remaining left elements form inversions with arr[j] */
            tmp[k++] = arr[j++];
        }
    }
    while (i < mid) tmp[k++] = arr[i++];
    while (j < hi)  tmp[k++] = arr[j++];
    for (int x = lo; x < hi; x++) arr[x] = tmp[x];

    return count;
}

long long count_inversions(int *arr, int n) {
    int tmp[n];
    return merge_count_inv(arr, tmp, 0, n);
}
⑥ Dry Run
arr = [2, 4, 1, 3, 5] Inversions: (2,1),(4,1),(4,3) → count = 3 Merge [2,4] and [1,3]: arr[0]=2 > tmp[0]=1 → count+=2 (both 2,4 > 1), place 1 arr[0]=2 < arr[1]=3 → no inv, place 2 arr[1]=4 > arr[1]=3 → count+=1, place 3, place 4 Total from this merge = 3 ✓
⚠ Gotcha: Adding 1 instead of (mid - i) when picking from the right

When you pick R[j] because L[i] > R[j], ALL remaining elements in L (positions i through mid-1) are greater than R[j] — because L is sorted. The inversion count to add is mid - i, not just 1. Adding 1 undercounts severely for large left sub-arrays.

315 · Count of Smaller Numbers After Self 493 · Reverse Pairs 912 · Sort an Array
When you see: count inversions (out-of-order pairs) in O(n log n) → think: merge sort + add (mid-i) to count when picking from right half
Pattern 54 Difference Array: Range Update in O(1)
① Story First
You need to add a value to every element in a range [l, r] — repeatedly, many times. Doing it naively updates O(r−l+1) elements each time. The difference array trick: only mark the start and end of each range update. Then reconstruct the actual values with a prefix sum at query time.
② The Insight
Store diff[l] += val and diff[r+1] -= val. Each range update is O(1). After all updates, the actual array is the prefix sum of diff[]. Then queries are O(n) but amortized over many updates.
⑤ Annotated Code
/* Difference array for multiple range updates.
   After all updates, call build() to get the final array values. */
int diff[10001] = {0};

/* Range update: add val to every element in arr[l..r] */
void range_update(int l, int r, int val) {
    diff[l]     += val;    /* range starts here: begin adding val */
    diff[r + 1] -= val;    /* range ends here: stop adding val */
}

/* Reconstruct final array by prefix-summing the difference array. */
void build(int *result, int n) {
    int running = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        running   += diff[i];   /* running sum accumulates all active range additions */
        result[i]  = running;
    }
}

/* Example: corporate flight bookings — seats booked per flight segment */
int max_seats(int bookings[][3], int nb, int n) {
    for (int i = 0; i <= n + 1; i++) diff[i] = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < nb; i++)
        range_update(bookings[i][0], bookings[i][1], bookings[i][2]);
    int result[n], max_val = 0;
    build(result, n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) if (result[i] > max_val) max_val = result[i];
    return max_val;
}
⑥ Dry Run
Updates on arr[0..4]: range_update(1, 3, +2) → diff[1]+=2, diff[4]-=2 range_update(0, 2, +1) → diff[0]+=1, diff[3]-=1 range_update(2, 4, +3) → diff[2]+=3, diff[5]-=3 diff = [1, 2, 3, -1, -2, -3] Build (prefix sum of diff): i=0: running=1 result[0]=1 i=1: running=3 result[1]=3 i=2: running=6 result[2]=6 i=3: running=5 result[3]=5 i=4: running=3 result[4]=3 result = [1, 3, 6, 5, 3] ✓
⚠ Gotcha: Writing diff[r] instead of diff[r+1] for the range end marker

The subtraction must go at index r+1, not r. You want val to be added for all positions l through r (inclusive). If you subtract at r, position r itself won't get val. The diff array requires diff[r+1] -= val so the prefix sum includes r but not r+1.

1109 · Corporate Flight Bookings 1094 · Car Pooling 370 · Range Addition
When you see: multiple range-add updates then query final values → think: difference array — mark start/end, reconstruct with prefix sum
